2 System Paradigms

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Architecture stack focus for this chapter.

Why does deploying the same model to a phone vs. a data center demand fundamentally different engineering?

The defining insight of ML systems engineering is that constraints drive architecture. The speed of light sets an absolute floor on how quickly distant servers can respond, thermodynamics limits how much computation can occur in a given volume before heat becomes unmanageable, and memory physics makes moving data more expensive than processing it. Because these physical limits persist as technology improves, deployment categories organize recurring constraint regimes rather than divide hardware into fixed, mutually exclusive classes. A data center can train billion-parameter models yet cannot guarantee low-latency responses to users thousands of miles away. A smartphone can respond immediately yet holds a fraction of the memory budget and must share a small battery with the rest of the phone. A microcontroller can run on a coin-cell battery for years while offering barely enough compute for a simple keyword detector. Across these regimes, power budgets span roughly nine orders of magnitude, from megawatts in a data center to milliwatts on a microcontroller, while memory capacity spans from terabytes to kilobytes. The same model applied to the same data therefore demands radically different engineering in each regime, not because the physics changes, but because different constraints dominate. The engineering task is not to identify a universally best deployment, but to locate the constraint that binds in the intended operating regime. In D·A·M terms, understanding these regimes turns deployment from an operational afterthought into a co-design problem in which data locality, algorithm structure, and machine constraints jointly determine what is possible.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain how physical constraints shape deployment paradigms from cloud to TinyML
  • Diagnose which term of a tier’s silicon contract binds a workload, separating iron-law limits from memory and energy feasibility
  • Match workload demands to deployment budgets using lighthouse model examples
  • Compare cloud, edge, mobile, and TinyML by operational constraints and quantitative trade-offs
  • Apply the decision framework to select paradigms by privacy, latency, resource, and cost constraints
  • Analyze hybrid patterns that combine paradigms to satisfy system constraints
  • Evaluate deployment placements against data-locality deadlines, energy per inference, and lifecycle cost
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

2.1 Four Deployment Regimes Across Nine Orders of Magnitude

The introduction’s D·A·M taxonomy (The D·A·M Intersection Landscape) treated Data, Algorithm, and Machine as three interacting dimensions of one design problem. Across real deployments, the machine is the dimension that changes most sharply, and its physical limits decide which data and algorithmic choices remain open. A wake-word detector on a smartwatch and a recommendation engine in a data center sit at opposite ends of that span: the wake-word detector operates inside milliwatt power budgets and kilobyte memory limits, whereas the recommendation engine draws on terabytes of embedding tables and megawatt-scale infrastructure.

The machine axis sets the deployment envelope.

Those physical constraints govern where a model can run in ways no algorithmic choice can override, and they bind the entire system rather than the model weights alone. Data ingestion pipelines, feature extraction, serving runtimes, telemetry, and hardware resource managers must all fit inside the host platform’s physical envelope.

Power falls from megawatts in the cloud to milliwatts in TinyML.

1 Deployment paradigm: A distinct operating regime defined by latency, power, memory, and connectivity constraints. The Cloud-to-TinyML spectrum changes which resources bind; optimizing within an infeasible regime cannot satisfy the application’s deployment budget. Raising a cloud model’s throughput by 5 percent, for example, cannot rescue an application whose 10 ms latency budget the network round trip alone exceeds.

Shi, Weisong, Jie Cao, Quan Zhang, Youhuizi Li, and Lanyu Xu. 2016. “Edge Computing: Vision and Challenges.” IEEE Internet of Things Journal 3 (5): 637–46. https://doi.org/10.1109/jiot.2016.2579198.

Three physical limits, namely latency, power dissipation, and memory, organize that span into four deployment paradigms.1 The categories overlap because edge describes physical proximity to data whereas mobile and TinyML distinguish local devices by their resource envelopes, and the pairing of a workload with its target platform determines which limit binds first. Centralizing computation in Cloud ML provides elastic compute and large memory capacity, at the cost of wide-area network latency on every inference. Moving workloads to Edge ML eliminates that network round trip and keeps sensitive data on premises (Shi et al. 2016), but it caps available compute at local site power and cooling budgets. Stepping onto battery-powered devices, Mobile ML delivers responsive interaction directly on handsets and tablets, within a passive thermal envelope where sustained computation triggers throttling. At the low-power extreme, TinyML runs on microcontrollers (Janapa Reddi et al. 2022) whose milliwatt power budgets and kilobyte memories require compact models and integer arithmetic. Along the way, the memory constraint changes form: in the data center it usually binds as bandwidth, whereas on a microcontroller it binds first as capacity. Arranged along one physical continuum, these tiers run from warehouse-scale megawatts in data centers down to milliwatts on embedded microcontrollers (figure 1).

While figure 1 traces the continuous progression from embedded sensors to data centers, table 1 quantifies conceptual operating envelopes across processing location, round-trip latency, power draw, and memory capacity. The cloud row describes facility power; the other rows describe local systems or devices. These envelopes compare deployment scales, whose physical basis is developed in section 2.2.

At every tier, the hardware offers a silicon contract: a compute rate, a memory bandwidth, and an execution overhead. Different tiers offer different budgets for these resources, and the contract also carries a memory capacity, which is not a term of execution time but a precondition that decides whether a workload fits on the tier at all. Physical constraints set those budgets, and each budget enters one term of the iron law of ML systems, which decomposes end-to-end execution time into data movement, computation, and system overhead. The speed of light, thermal dissipation, and memory signaling shape those terms, which is why deployment paradigms exist as distinct engineering regimes rather than arbitrary points on a continuous slider.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Which statement correctly characterizes the span of power and memory capacity across the four deployment paradigms (Cloud ML, Edge ML, Mobile ML, and TinyML)?

    1. Power spans approximately nine orders of magnitude from megawatts in cloud data centers to milliwatts in microcontrollers, while memory spans from terabytes to kilobytes.
    2. Power spans three orders of magnitude from kilowatts to watts, while memory remains in the gigabyte range across all four deployment tiers.
    3. Power is identical across Edge, Mobile, and TinyML tiers (all operating under 5 W), with only Cloud ML exceeding 100 W.
    4. Memory capacity varies by at most a factor of \(10^3\) between cloud servers and microcontrollers, while power varies by \(10^{12}\).

  2. In ML systems engineering, what is meant by the silicon contract established by a single-node stack?

    1. The legal warranty provided by chip foundries guaranteeing zero hardware defects during multi-week training runs.
    2. The fixed performance bargain offered by a hardware platform, defined by its peak compute rate, memory bandwidth, and fixed launch overhead.
    3. The software abstraction layer that guarantees automated portability across CPUs, GPUs, and microcontrollers without performance regression.
    4. The dynamic power-scaling policy that allows an accelerator to exceed its thermal design power indefinitely under heavy batch loads.

  3. Why does deploying an ML model across different paradigms require treating deployment as a first-order D·A·M co-design problem rather than a late-stage packaging step?

See Answers →

2.2 Physical Constraints on Deployment

A safety system with a 10 ms reaction budget cannot wait for a cross-country round trip, and a billion-parameter model cannot be squeezed into a microcontroller by better code alone. Both follow directly from the physical laws governing the speed of light, power thermodynamics, and memory signaling. The light barrier limits remote responsiveness, the power wall restricts sustained throughput, and the memory wall caps data transfer. Where a system runs changes the operational terms the hardware offers, which changes what the hardware can offer the model.

↩︎ A first sketch of the deployment spectrum and its three walls is established in Deployment context shapes the lifecycle.

The light barrier

Sending a sensor packet from California to a cloud data center in Virginia (~3,600 km straight-line) requires ~36 ms round-trip in optical fiber before a single floating-point operation begins, and real networks add routing, protocol, queuing, and processing delays on top. Any application that requires a sub-10 ms response cannot rely on distant cloud infrastructure, no matter how fast the remote accelerators are. The light barrier sets this absolute latency2 floor. Let \(\text{Distance}\) denote the one-way path length and \(\FiberPropagationSpeed\) the propagation speed in optical fiber. Equation 1 gives the minimum round-trip time: \[\MinimumRoundTripLatency= \frac{2 \times \text{Distance}}{\FiberPropagationSpeed} \approx \frac{2 \times \text{Distance}}{200{,}000 \text{ km/s}} \tag{1}\] For optical fiber, \(\FiberPropagationSpeed\approx 200{,}000\) km/s, roughly two-thirds of its vacuum value because light travels more slowly through glass than through a vacuum. This propagation floor directly motivates edge ML: when a tight latency budget rules out the round trip, computation must move closer to the data source.

2 Latency: The time between issuing a request and receiving a result, corresponding to \(\Time\) in the iron law, where propagation delay enters through the fixed-overhead term \(\Latency\). The light barrier makes that propagation floor irreducible, as equation 1 quantifies. Every millisecond consumed by distance is a millisecond unavailable for model inference, which is why the light barrier forces paradigm selection rather than mere optimization.

The power wall

Doubling a processor’s clock frequency to finish a model’s arithmetic in half the time does not merely double its power draw. Higher frequencies typically require higher supply voltages to switch transistors reliably, so dynamic power can jump roughly eightfold, and every extra watt becomes heat that the package and chassis must carry away. That thermal ceiling is the power wall, which limits how much computation can occur in a given volume. Dynamic CMOS power follows equation 2, where \(\SwitchedCapacitance\) is capacitance, \(\SupplyVoltage\) is supply voltage, and \(\ClockFrequency\) is clock frequency. Under the illustrative assumption that voltage rises proportionally with frequency (\(\SupplyVoltage\propto \ClockFrequency\)), dynamic power rises cubically (\(\text{Power} \propto \ClockFrequency^3\)). The exact voltage-frequency relationship varies by device, but increasing both consumes thermal headroom rapidly. \[\text{Power} \propto \SwitchedCapacitance\times \SupplyVoltage^2 \times \ClockFrequency\quad \text{where } \SupplyVoltage\propto \ClockFrequency\implies \text{Power} \propto \ClockFrequency^3 \tag{2}\]

When broad voltage scaling broke down in the mid-2000s,3 that cubic penalty ended “free” frequency speedups and forced hardware designers toward parallelism (multi-core CPUs) and domain-specific specialization (GPUs and Tensor Processing Units (TPUs)4). Figure 2 records that turn across five decades of processors. From the mid-2000s to the early 2020s the largest chips gained about 190× more transistors, yet the fastest clock stayed near 4.7 GHz and single-thread performance rose only 5×, against 21× in the decade before. The added transistors went into more cores and specialized units rather than a faster single thread.

3 Dennard scaling: Named after Robert Dennard, whose IBM team described MOSFET scaling relationships under which shrinking devices could reduce voltage and current while keeping power density approximately controlled (Dennard et al. 1974). As voltage scaling slowed and energy became a first-order architectural constraint, performance growth shifted toward parallelism and specialization: multi-core processors, GPUs, and TPUs (Hennessy and Patterson 2019; Esmaeilzadeh et al. 2011).

Dennard, Robert H., Frank H. Gaensslen, Hwa-Nien Yu, Victor L. Rideout, Elias Bassous, and Antoine R. LeBlanc. 1974. “Design of Ion-Implanted MOSFET’s with Very Small Physical Dimensions.” IEEE J. Solid-State Circuits 9 (5): 256–68. https://doi.org/10.1109/jssc.1974.1050511.
Hennessy, John L., and David A. Patterson. 2019. “A New Golden Age for Computer Architecture.” Communications of the ACM 62 (2): 48–60. https://doi.org/10.1145/3282307.
Esmaeilzadeh, Hadi, Emily Blem, Renee St. Amant, Karthikeyan Sankaralingam, and Doug Burger. 2011. “Dark Silicon and the End of Multicore Scaling.” Proceedings of the 38th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture, 365–76. https://doi.org/10.1145/2000064.2000108.

4 Tensor processing unit (TPU): An application-specific integrated circuit (ASIC) designed for operations common in ML, especially matrix multiplication. PFLOP/s-to-EFLOP/s figures describe aggregated TPU systems rather than one chip. Specialization trades general-purpose flexibility for workload-dependent efficiency; its advantage over a GPU depends on the model, numerical format, and systems being compared (Jouppi et al. 2023).

Jouppi, Norm, George Kurian, Sheng Li, Peter Ma, Rahul Nagarajan, Lifeng Nai, Nishant Patil, et al. 2023. “TPU v4: An Optically Reconfigurable Supercomputer for Machine Learning with Hardware Support for Embeddings.” Proceedings of the 50th Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture, 1–14. https://doi.org/10.1145/3579371.3589350.

The same limit is tightest on mobile devices, which hit a thermal ceiling at 3–5 W; exceeding it causes “throttling,” where the device reduces performance to prevent overheating, a trace worked through in notebook 2.9. This physical limit gives rise to mobile ML: battery-powered devices cannot simply run cloud-scale models locally.

The memory wall

An accelerator’s matrix units can finish a block of multiplications in nanoseconds, yet spend most of an inference step sitting idle while weights and activations trickle in from off-chip DRAM. The memory wall (Wulf and McKee 1995) names this widening gap between processor speed and memory delivery, which shows up in ML chiefly as a bandwidth5 shortfall. A rough sketch with representative annual growth factors shows why the gap compounds: \[\frac{\text{Compute Growth Rate}}{\text{Memory Bandwidth Growth Rate}} \approx \frac{1.6}{1.2} \approx 1.33 \tag{3}\]

5 Memory bandwidth (the memory wall): The term “memory wall” comes from Wulf and McKee (1995), who predicted that the processor-memory performance gap would eventually dominate system performance, a prediction borne out for ML workloads in which weight or activation movement can bind performance. Whether bandwidth binds a given workload depends on arithmetic intensity, batch size, data reuse, access pattern, and hardware balance rather than model size alone.

Wulf, Wm. A., and Sally A. McKee. 1995. “Hitting the Memory Wall: Implications of the Obvious.” ACM SIGARCH Computer Architecture News 23 (1): 20–24. https://doi.org/10.1145/216585.216588.

In equation 3, the numerator and denominator are dimensionless annual growth factors. A ratio above 1 means compute capability is pulling away from memory bandwidth, so each hardware generation makes data movement a larger share of the performance problem unless the workload increases locality or arithmetic intensity (operations performed per byte moved).

Compute capacity outruns memory bandwidth; the widening gap is the memory wall.

Horowitz, Mark. 2014. “1.1 Computing’s Energy Problem (and What We Can Do about It).” 2014 IEEE International Solid-State Circuits Conference Digest of Technical Papers (ISSCC), 10–14. https://doi.org/10.1109/isscc.2014.6757323.

This illustrative trend comparison assumes that compute capacity doubles every 18 months, about 1.6× per year (the numerator of equation 3), while memory bandwidth grows by ~20 percent annually (the denominator). These assumed rates illustrate the widening gap rather than a forecast for every hardware generation. Sustained for a decade, those rates leave compute about 16× further ahead of bandwidth than at the start. This widening gap makes data movement a frequent bottleneck, in both time and energy, for ML workloads. An on-chip arithmetic logic unit (ALU) operates over short local connections, whereas an off-chip DRAM access also drives memory interfaces and longer interconnects. In representative hardware, fetching operands from DRAM can cost orders of magnitude more energy than the arithmetic performed on them (Horowitz 2014). The ratio depends on the operation, numerical precision, memory technology, and amount of data transferred. The constraint affects all paradigms, and it takes a different form on TinyML devices, where capacity, not bandwidth, is usually exhausted first.

↪ The architectural responses to the memory wall, high-bandwidth memory (HBM) and on-chip SRAM hierarchies, are developed in Memory hierarchy.

Checkpoint 2.1: Physical constraints and deployment

Deployment choices are governed by physics, not just preference. Check your understanding:

These physical laws explain the trade-offs behind the four paradigms. Physical limits decide which designs can work at all; privacy regulation, economic incentives, and data sovereignty requirements further restrict the choices within those limits. No regulation can make the speed of light faster, and no economic model can repeal thermodynamics. Nor do those walls act in isolation: in practice, an ML system is limited by whichever physical boundary binds on its primary resource first. Evaluating these limits quantitatively requires analytical tools: the iron law to decompose execution time into its components, the bottleneck principle to identify the rate-limiting stage, and workload archetypes to classify the model’s resource demands.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A safety-critical autonomous braking system requires an end-to-end decision latency budget of 10 ms. A cloud data center is located 1,500 km away. Given that light travels through optical fiber at approximately \(200{,}000\text{ km/s}\), why is offloading inference to this cloud data center physically impossible?

    1. The cloud server queue latency always adds at least 25 ms regardless of server load.
    2. The speed of light in fiber allows a round trip in 5 ms, but serialization of model weights requires 15 ms.
    3. The minimum speed-of-light round-trip propagation time alone is 15 ms, consuming the entire 10 ms budget before compute begins.
    4. Wireless transmission protocols require a minimum 50 ms handshake before transmitting sensor packets.

  2. Under dynamic CMOS power scaling where supply voltage scales proportionally with clock frequency (\(V \propto f\)), doubling the processor clock frequency increases dynamic power dissipation by a factor of 8 (\(f^3\)).

  3. How does the memory wall manifest in modern computing hardware according to the historical growth rates of compute capacity versus memory bandwidth?

    1. Memory bandwidth doubles every 18 months while compute throughput increases by only 20% annually, causing compute starvation.
    2. Both compute throughput and memory bandwidth grow at identical 60% annual rates, but memory capacity limits model size.
    3. Processors have reached absolute thermal limits while memory signaling energy has dropped to near-zero levels.
    4. Compute capacity historically doubles roughly every 18 months (~60% annually), whereas memory bandwidth grows by only ~20% annually, causing the compute-to-bandwidth gap to widen compounding over time.

  4. What causes thermal throttling on passively cooled mobile devices running sustained ML inference, and how does it affect continuous model execution?

  5. The historical scaling relationship under which shrinking MOSFET transistor dimensions allowed reducing voltage and current while keeping power density constant is known as ____ scaling.

See Answers →

2.3 Analyzing Workloads

Which of the three physical walls binds first is not a fixed property of a model family. A vision network that saturates accelerator compute units during batched training can stall on memory bandwidth when serving one image at a time, and a model that runs effortlessly in a data center can overheat a phone in under a minute. Turning those physical limits into a quantitative diagnosis for any pairing of workload and target hardware requires the iron law of ML systems from Iron Law of ML Systems, restated here in the same notation because every tier in this chapter is analyzed through it. For a serialized execution path, equation 4 adds three costs: \[\Time= \frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth} + \frac{\Operations}{\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw} + \Latency \tag{4}\]

Equation 4 decomposes total latency into three terms: data movement \((\DataVolume/\Bandwidth)\), compute \((\Operations/(\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw))\), and fixed overhead \((\Latency)\). A workload is compute bound when its compute term \(\Operations/(\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw)\) exceeds its data term \(\DataVolume/\Bandwidth\), and bandwidth-bound in the reverse case, an outcome that depends on the workload, its execution conditions, and the deployment hardware. For an inference path that executes these stages sequentially, their times add, whereas a single request with overlapping work must follow the actual dependencies. In production systems, however, tasks are processed continuously as a stream, and the analysis shifts from single-task latency to identifying which stage limits steady-state throughput.

2.3.1 The bottleneck principle

Adding a twice-as-fast accelerator to a serving pipeline does nothing for steady-state throughput if the processor spends half of every step waiting for the memory bus to deliver the next batch of weights. Where the iron law quantifies the cost of each stage, the bottleneck principle identifies which stage governs continuous throughput. Iron Law of ML Systems gave the overlapped form of the iron law for pipelined execution, in which the memory system prefetches batch \(\BatchIndex+1\) while the accelerator computes on batch \(\BatchIndex\). The slowest stage then hides the others and sets the interval between completions, so speeding a nonlimiting stage does not raise ideal steady-state throughput while the system bottleneck remains unchanged. Deployment adds one more overlapped stage, \(\IoTransferTime\), the time to transfer data over a storage or network link, including offloading a request to a remote server. Equation 5 extends the pipelined form with that stage. Here the left-hand side is the steady-state time per completed task, with fixed overhead treated as nonoverlapped; equality requires ideal overlap of the other stages: \[ \PipelinedExecutionTime\ge \max\left(\frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth}, \frac{\Operations}{\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw}, \IoTransferTime\right) + \Latency \tag{5}\]

When a system is bandwidth-bound \((\DataVolume/\Bandwidth > \Operations/(\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw))\), faster processors \((\PeakRate)\) do not raise ideal pipeline throughput while memory remains the slowest stage, just as widening a highway cannot help traffic constrained by a narrow bridge. Shortening that compute stage still reduces an individual request’s start-to-finish latency, because a single request must finish loading its own inputs before its arithmetic can complete, even when the pipeline overlaps that arithmetic with the memory transfer of the next request. Engineers must therefore identify both the metric that matters and the dominant term before optimizing. The additive form in equation 4 governs a single task’s serialized path, whereas the max form in equation 5 governs the completion interval of an ideally overlapped continuous stream, and the actual data dependencies determine which stages can overlap.

2.3.2 Workload archetypes

The bottleneck principle reduces optimization to identifying the dominant constraint for a given workload. The answer depends on all three D·A·M axes at once: the data a request carries, the algorithm’s arithmetic and memory demands, and the machine that executes it. Different deployment environments create different bottlenecks along these axes, so the same model family can require different engineering when it moves from a cloud server with terabytes of memory to a microcontroller with kilobytes. Classifying workloads by what binds them first yields four workload archetypes.6 Each archetype is a recurring physical bottleneck that determines which engineering moves can help. Two of them correspond to terms of the iron law. The other two are bound by preconditions the time equation assumes are already met, namely that the working set fits in memory and that the energy budget can pay for the run. A capacity or energy limit can therefore decide feasibility before any term of equation 4 is evaluated.

6 Workload archetype: A classification of ML workloads by their dominant bottleneck rather than by their model family. Misidentifying the archetype wastes optimization effort on the wrong term of the iron law, as when teams add accelerator FLOP/s to a bandwidth-bound inference pipeline and obtain little improvement while memory transfer remains dominant.

The first split, between the two iron-law terms, separates arithmetic-bound systems from data-movement-bound systems. A compute-bound workload performs many calculations per byte loaded, so progress comes from higher arithmetic throughput, better utilization, and more parallel execution; large neural-network training is the canonical case. A bandwidth-bound workload, by contrast, waits on dense weight or activation movement, so wider memory buses and better data reuse matter more than additional peak FLOP/s; autoregressive text generation illustrates this regime. The roofline literature calls this regime memory-bound; this book says bandwidth-bound to keep it distinct from running out of memory capacity, which is the next archetype.

The second split covers workloads where the binding constraint is not a term of the time equation at all. Capacity-bound workloads are dominated by irregular table lookups and poor cache locality, so memory capacity, access latency, and communication shape performance in recommendation systems with large embedding tables. Power-bound workloads face the opposite envelope: energy per inference and memory footprint, not raw speed, determine whether always-on sensing can run at all.

Lighthouse 2.1: Five reference workloads

The introduction (Lighthouse models put the iron law into practice) introduced five lighthouse models by the system bottleneck each isolates (Lighthouse Models as Reference Workloads); table 2 pairs each with a representative workload archetype and deployment paradigm. The classifications describe operating regimes: batched image processing can emphasize arithmetic, single-request text generation can emphasize weight transfers, and a large recommendation table can exceed local memory capacity. Changing batch size, model size, or hardware can change the binding constraint. MobileNetV2 shows why. In table 2, it sits with the compute-oriented vision workloads because its pointwise convolutions carry most of its arithmetic, sized down to fit mobile thermal and energy envelopes. Its depthwise layers, however, perform few operations per byte and are often bandwidth-bound on mobile accelerators. Whether arithmetic throughput or memory bandwidth ultimately binds MobileNetV2 on a specific device depends on target hardware balance, precision, and batching, as the roofline analyses in section 2.4 demonstrate. Network Architectures opens up the architecture behind each lighthouse.

Each archetype gravitates toward the tier that relieves its primary bottleneck, starting in the cloud where compute-bound training, bandwidth-bound decoding of large models, and capacity-bound tables pool data-center resources. The image classifier ResNet-50 anchors the compute-bound regime, spending approximately 8.2 GFLOP per image on 25.6 million parameters that occupy 102.2 MB at FP32 (He et al. 2016). That regular, compute-dense structure makes it the canonical benchmark for hardware accelerator performance.

He, Kaiming, Xiangyu Zhang, Shaoqing Ren, and Jian Sun. 2016. “Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition.” 2016 IEEE Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 770–78. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2016.90.
Radford, Alec, Jeffrey Wu, Rewon Child, David Luan, Dario Amodei, and Ilya Sutskever. 2019. Language Models Are Unsupervised Multitask Learners. OpenAI.
Touvron, Hugo, Thibaut Lavril, Gautier Izacard, Xavier Martinet, Marie-Anne Lachaux, Timothée Lacroix, Baptiste Rozière, et al. 2023. “LLaMA: Open and Efficient Foundation Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2302.13971.
Touvron, Hugo, Louis Martin, Kevin Stone, Peter Albert, Amjad Almahairi, Yasmine Babaei, Nikolay Bashlykov, et al. 2023. “Llama 2: Open Foundation and Fine-Tuned Chat Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.09288.
Pope, Reiner, Sholto Douglas, Aakanksha Chowdhery, Jacob Devlin, James Bradbury, Jonathan Heek, Kefan Xiao, Shivani Agrawal, and Jeff Dean. 2023. “Efficiently Scaling Transformer Inference.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 5: 606–24.

Where ResNet-50 reuses weights across spatial positions and batches, the language models GPT-2/Llama shift the cloud bottleneck from arithmetic to memory bandwidth during autoregressive text generation (Radford et al. 2019; Touvron, Lavril, et al. 2023; Touvron, Martin, et al. 2023). Because multi-billion parameter models exceed on-chip cache, weights reside in external memory, and generating text one token at a time for a single request forces the processor to stream those weights across the memory bus on every token with little reuse (1.5 billion parameters for GPT-2, 7 billion–70 billion parameters for Llama). This sequential access pattern creates an autoregressive bottleneck that shapes serving cost (Pope et al. 2023), keeping large-model decoding in the cloud unless a smaller variant fits locally.

↪ Continuous batching and KV-cache paging, the runtime mechanisms that serve token-by-token generation, are developed in Continuous batching.

7 Deep learning recommendation model (DLRM): Meta’s 2019 architecture that exemplifies the capacity-bound archetype. At that scale, memory capacity and communication \(\Bandwidth\) can constrain execution more than raw \(\PeakRate\). These demands motivate specialized cluster designs where memory, not arithmetic, is the scarce resource.

Naumov, Maxim, Dheevatsa Mudigere, Hao-Jun Michael Shi, Jianyu Huang, Narayanan Sundaraman, Jongsoo Park, Xiaodong Wang, et al. 2019. “Deep Learning Recommendation Model for Personalization and Recommendation Systems.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1906.00091.

A third cloud workload hits the memory precondition before bandwidth or arithmetic can bind. The recommendation lighthouse, DLRM7, represents the embedding-heavy recommendation workload behind large-scale “You might also like” systems (Naumov et al. 2019). It maps users and items to embedding vectors stored in tables that can exceed 100 GB of embeddings, making memory capacity rather than computation the binding constraint.

Moving from data centers to edge and mobile devices requires shrinking those resource demands by orders of magnitude. The mobile lighthouse, MobileNetV2, performs the same image classification task as ResNet-50 while cutting its computation by 13.7× (Sandler et al. 2018). It gets there by restructuring the image-filtering layers, splitting each expensive filtering step into two much cheaper ones (depthwise separable convolutions). That reduction makes it a candidate for real-time inference within a smartphone’s 3–5 W envelope; the implementation and workload determine whether it meets the deadline.

Sandler, Mark, Andrew Howard, Menglong Zhu, Andrey Zhmoginov, and Liang-Chieh Chen. 2018. “MobileNetV2: Inverted Residuals and Linear Bottlenecks.” 2018 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition, 4510–20. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2018.00474.

↪ How depthwise separable convolutions factor one filtering step into two cheaper ones is developed in Depthwise separable convolutions.

Zhang, Yundong, Naveen Suda, Liangzhen Lai, and Vikas Chandra. 2017. Hello Edge: Keyword Spotting on Microcontrollers.

Stepping down once more to microcontrollers puts power and kilobyte memory first. The TinyML lighthouse, keyword spotting (KWS), represents the always-on sensing archetype, detecting short trigger phrases with compact models built for resource-constrained microcontrollers (Zhang et al. 2017). The lighthouse workload has approximately 200K parameters, about 200 KB at INT8 (stored in flash), placing it in the kilobyte-memory, milliwatt-budget TinyML regime.

Checking whether such a constrained workload can run at all requires testing the capacity precondition before evaluating the iron law’s time terms. A smart doorbell, a TinyML scenario that recurs through later chapters alongside the five lighthouses, applies the always-on pattern to vision and illustrates this two-level screen, checking storage first and latency second. A visual wake-word model (Chowdhery et al. 2019), a small classifier that answers only whether a person is in view, runs on an ESP32-S3 microcontroller, the TinyML platform of table 4. At level 1, the 0.5 MB of 16-bit weights occupy 11.9 percent of the 4.2 MB flash. Passing this storage check does not establish complete memory fit, because intermediate results and runtime state must also fit in SRAM, the two-tier split that section 2.8.1 develops. Level 2 applies the iron law. The model needs 25 MFLOP per frame, and the microcontroller sustains about 0.25 GFLOP/s at this precision, so compute takes 100 ms; about 1 ms of fixed overhead brings the total to 101 ms. That estimate clears the scenario’s canonical 200 ms budget; sensor acquisition and any data movement omitted by the estimate must still be checked. A more responsive chime with a 50 ms budget changes the verdict without changing the model, and figure 3 plots that tightened case. In the plot, flash stays far below the saturation line while latency reaches 202 percent of its budget. Flash storage was not the binding constraint; latency is, and on-chip SRAM still has to be checked separately.

Chowdhery, Aakanksha et al. 2019. “Visual Wake Words Dataset.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1906.05721.

Even on one microcontroller, tightening the deadline flips the binding constraint from storage to compute time, and across the full lighthouse set the resource demands diverge far more sharply. A keyword spotter operates with roughly 20 MFLOP per inference and about 200 KB of INT8 weights, whereas ResNet-50 requires about 8.2 GFLOP per image and roughly 102.2 MB of FP32 weights. The reference DLRM example already reaches 100 GB, and Meta has demonstrated scaling recommendation models to 100 TB using embedding offloading (Park et al. 2024), while language models stream billions of parameters repeatedly from memory during autoregressive inference. Whether each of these demands passes its capacity and timing checks depends on the compute, bandwidth, and memory a given platform supplies.

Park, Intaik, Ehsan Ardestani, Damian Reeves, Sarunya Pumma, Henry Tsang, Levy Zhao, Jian He, et al. 2024. “Toward 100TB Recommendation Models with Embedding Offloading.” 18th ACM Conference on Recommender Systems, 841–43. https://doi.org/10.1145/3640457.3688037.
Self-Check: Question

  1. When analyzing ML system performance, when should an engineer apply the additive form of the iron law (\(T = \frac{D_{\text{vol}}}{\text{BW}} + \frac{O}{R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}}} + L_{\text{lat}}\)) versus the bottleneck principle’s max form (\(T_{\text{pipelined}} \ge \max\left(\frac{D_{\text{vol}}}{\text{BW}}, \frac{O}{R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}}}, T_{\text{IO}}\right) + L_{\text{lat}}\))?

    1. Use the additive form for single-request serialized latency, and the max form for steady-state pipelined completion interval after the pipeline fills, where overlapped stages are bounded by the slowest one.
    2. Use the additive form exclusively for cloud workloads, and the max form exclusively for microcontroller TinyML workloads.
    3. Use the additive form when batch size is greater than 128, and the max form when batch size equals 1.
    4. Use the additive form during model training only, and the max form during on-device model quantization.

  2. Which pairing correctly matches a workload archetype to its dominant physical bottleneck and canonical lighthouse model?

    1. Compute-bound \(\rightarrow\) dominated by irregular table lookups and cache misses \(\rightarrow\) DLRM
    2. Bandwidth-bound \(\rightarrow\) dominated by dense parameter streaming during autoregressive token decode \(\rightarrow\) GPT-2/Llama
    3. Capacity-bound \(\rightarrow\) dominated by dense FLOP throughput at high arithmetic intensity \(\rightarrow\) ResNet-50
    4. Power-bound \(\rightarrow\) dominated by petabyte-scale distributed training \(\rightarrow\) Keyword Spotting (KWS)

  3. Explain why shortening a stage that is not the bottleneck can still reduce the latency of one sequential request, even though it does not improve ideal pipelined throughput once the pipeline is full.

  4. Order the steps of the hierarchical deployment feasibility evaluation for the Smart Doorbell scenario from initial problem framing to latency verification:

  1. Check Level 1 storage fit by screening whether the visual wake-word model’s weights fit in flash
  2. Define the application mission targets (person at the door, required quality, and responsiveness budget)
  3. Check Level 2 timing constraints by evaluating per-inference latency against the interaction budget
  4. Select a candidate platform and model for the task (for example, a visual wake-word model on an ESP32-S3)
  1. An ML workload characterized by high arithmetic intensity where throughput is bounded by peak FLOP/s (\(R_{\text{peak}}\)) rather than memory bandwidth, such as dense convolutional neural network training, belongs to the ____ archetype.

See Answers →

2.4 System Balance and Hardware

A model that clears its latency budget on paper can still stall on a real host if PCIe transfers between CPU and GPU memory choke the pipeline or if single-sample inference leaves the accelerator’s matrix units starved for bytes. Checking the iron law and workload archetypes against real hardware means tracing a request through every layer of one host, from the application’s latency or throughput target down to the silicon that must meet it.

2.4.1 Inside one machine: The single-node stack

When an application issues an inference request or a training step on a single machine, that work travels down a four-layer single-node stack, which refines the machine side of the D·A·M taxonomy for one host. At the top of the stack, the application brings the data and algorithmic requirements into a training-throughput or inference-latency target, and the ML framework below it translates model code into executable operations that aim to meet that target. Those operations then pass through the operating system, drivers, and runtime, which coordinate resources and move tensors between host memory and accelerator memory. At the bottom is the hardware itself, where HBM capacity, memory bandwidth, intra-node interconnects such as NVLink, and compute throughput set the physical limits, with the memory wall as a recurring constraint.

↪ How an ML framework turns model code into executed operations, eagerly or through a captured graph, is developed in Execution: From Graphs to Kernels.

The hardware layer fixes the terms on offer to any model: a memory bandwidth \((\Bandwidth)\), a peak compute rate \((\PeakRate)\), and a fixed overhead \((\Latency)\). These are the terms of the silicon contract. The hardware offers them, and the workload and its execution conditions determine which resource limits progress first. Deployment analysis works with the hardware’s half, asking which term a given platform makes scarce; the model architecture’s half, the choice of which resource to saturate, belongs to Network Architectures.

↩︎ The iron law of ML systems and its decomposition of end-to-end latency are established in Iron Law of ML Systems.

2.4.2 Matching workloads to hardware

Testing whether a host’s silicon contract can satisfy a workload proceeds in two steps, from coarse to fine, before any kernel-level profiling begins. The coarse test asks whether an operation can fit inside the latency budget at all. The fine test asks which hardware resource limits the operations that do fit, and its answer depends on system balance, the ratio of peak compute throughput to memory bandwidth and I/O capacity on a given platform.

Operation latencies supply the coarse test, and they turn latency-vs-throughput trade-offs into decisions through concrete numbers. They span roughly eight orders of magnitude, from about a nanosecond for an on-chip cache hit or a single accelerator multiply, through milliseconds for one mobile vision inference, to tens or hundreds of milliseconds for a cross-region network call. Numbers to Know collects the memory-hierarchy and network round-trip anchors along with illustrative application latency budgets. The span matters because of one strict rule: an operation with latency \(> \CompletionDeadline\) cannot appear on the critical path of a system whose latency budget is \(\CompletionDeadline\) ms.8

8 Critical path: The longest sequential chain of dependent operations in a pipeline. The decision rule in the triggering sentence is strict: if a 200 ms cross-region network call appears anywhere on the critical path, a system with a 100 ms total budget is guaranteed to fail regardless of how fast every other stage runs. Model inference need not be the longest stage; preprocessing and postprocessing can dominate, making the critical path longer than model execution alone suggests.

In this example, batching moves the workload from the memory-bound side toward the compute ceiling.

Williams, Samuel, Andrew Waterman, and David Patterson. 2009. “Roofline: An Insightful Visual Performance Model for Multicore Architectures.” Communications of the ACM 52 (4): 65–76. https://doi.org/10.1145/1498765.1498785.

For the operations that pass the latency test, system balance decides which resource limits them. Roofline analysis classifies bottlenecks by comparing a workload’s arithmetic intensity, the operations it performs per byte moved (FLOP/byte), against the hardware machine balance point \((\PeakRate / \Bandwidth)\), the ridge point (Williams et al. 2009) introduced with the iron law in Iron Law of ML Systems. The framing is informal here; Performance Bounds develops the model and the ridge point that separates the memory-bound and compute-bound regimes, with a worked derivation collected for reference in The Roofline model. In that framing, the same ResNet-50 model can shift from compute-bound training behavior at high batch sizes to more memory-sensitive single-image inference at batch=1. Deployment paradigm selection must account for this shift.

The batch-size shift is one instance of a broader change between execution phases, in which physical constraints reconfigure all three D·A·M axes at once. Training updates model weights from batches of examples; serving applies fixed weights to new inputs. Table 3 contrasts typical training and latency-sensitive serving demands; cloud serving can also batch requests for throughput.

ResNet-50 inference on a high-end data center accelerator and on a mobile neural processing unit (NPU)9 puts numbers on this analysis. The hardware figures are point-in-time anchors; the reasoning rests on compute rate, memory bandwidth, and batch size, which outlast any one product.

9 Neural processing unit (NPU): A dedicated hardware block on a mobile system-on-chip whose circuits are designed for low-precision tensor operations. This specialization can improve energy efficiency relative to CPU execution, helping AI workloads fit within mobile power budgets.

Napkin Math 2.1: ResNet-50 on cloud vs. mobile

Problem: Is ResNet-50 inference compute-bound or bandwidth-bound on (a) a high-end data center accelerator (an NVIDIA A100) and (b) a flagship mobile NPU?

Given (from lighthouse models):

  • ResNet-50: 8.2 GFLOP per inference, 25.6 million parameters (102.2 MB FP32, 51.1 MB FP16)

Analysis:

(a) Cloud data-center accelerator (batch=1, FP16)

The analytical model assumes 56.2 MB moved per inference, including weights and activation traffic. This is a modeled transfer volume, not the stored parameter footprint; reuse and runtime choices can change it.

  • Peak compute: 312 TFLOP/s (FP16)
  • Memory bandwidth: 2.04 TB/s
  • Compute time: \(\IronLawComputeTime\) = \(\frac{8.20 \times 10^{9}}{3.12 \times 10^{14}}\) = 0.026 ms
  • Memory time (weights plus activations): \(\IronLawMemoryTime\) = \(\frac{5.62 \times 10^{7}}{2.04 \times 10^{12}}\) = 0.028 ms
  • Result: Bottleneck = Memory (1.0× slower than compute)
  • Ridge point: \(\PeakRate/\Bandwidth\) = 312 TFLOP/s ÷ 2.04 TB/s ≈ 153 FLOP/byte.
  • Analysis: Arithmetic intensity = \(\frac{8.20 \times 10^{9}}{5.62 \times 10^{7}}\) = 146 FLOP/byte, just below the ridge point, so the workload is bandwidth-bound; above the ridge it would be compute bound. For single-image inference, the low batch size yields limited reuse, explaining why even high-throughput accelerators can be bandwidth-bound at batch = 1. Both times use peak rates (efficiency \(\EfficiencyHw = 1\) and full bandwidth), so they are lower bounds. Because this workload sits close to the ridge, the efficiencies a real kernel achieves decide which term dominates in practice.

(b) Mobile: Flagship NPU (batch=1, INT8)

At INT8, the analytical model assumes 28.1 MB moved per inference, including weights and activation traffic. The INT8 weight footprint below is only one component of that transfer volume.

  • Peak compute: ~35 TOPS (INT8), representative of modern mobile NPUs
  • Memory bandwidth: ~51.2 GB/s (LPDDR5)
  • Model size: 25.6 MB (INT8 quantized)
  • Compute time: \(\IronLawComputeTime\) = \(\frac{8.20 \times 10^{9} \text{ INT8 ops}}{3.50 \times 10^{13} \text{ INT8 ops/s}}\) = 0.23 ms
  • Memory time (weights plus activations): \(\IronLawMemoryTime\) = \(\frac{2.81 \times 10^{7}}{5.12 \times 10^{10}}\) = 0.55 ms
  • Result: Bottleneck = Memory (2.3× slower than compute)

Systems insight: Under these peak-rate assumptions, both platforms are bandwidth-bound for single-image inference. The A100’s faster memory bandwidth (2.04 TB/s vs. 51.2 GB/s = 39.8×) gives roughly 19.9× lower modeled memory-transfer time under the stated FP16 and INT8 traffic assumptions; this ratio is not a measured end-to-end inference speedup. Byte-reduction and lower-precision techniques can therefore beat buying more peak FLOP/s for deployment.

ResNet-50 becomes compute bound when batching and data reuse raise computation per byte moved above the hardware balance point, \(\ArithmeticIntensity > \PeakRate/\Bandwidth\), where \(\ArithmeticIntensity = \Operations/\DataVolume\). With ideal reuse during batched inference, each weight is transferred across the memory bus once and used across all \(\BatchSize\) samples in the batch. This amortizes the weight transfer over \(\BatchSize\) inputs, increasing the arithmetic intensity until computation time overtakes memory-transfer time. The crossover is architecture- and implementation-dependent because activation traffic, input/output movement, cache reuse, and runtime execution details all change the effective bytes moved per inference.

Extending that cloud-versus-mobile comparison across all four deployment tiers grounds the conceptual regimes of table 1 in concrete hardware. Table 4 places one real platform in each paradigm and pairs it with the per-device envelope that marks the tier.10 The example-platform and power columns describe complete systems, including a cloud pod, while the tier compute and bandwidth columns describe per-device envelopes. Because vendors quote peak rates at the numerical precisions their chips target, the per-device compute column must be read alongside its format: the cloud floor states dense 16-bit floating-point throughput per accelerator, whereas the edge ceiling reflects 4-bit sparse operations on a high-end workstation. At matched precision and density, one TPU v4 chip still delivers 2.2× the dense 16-bit throughput and 4.4× the memory bandwidth of the DGX Spark. The following spans compare the complete system examples rather than equivalent individual chips. Memory drops from 128 TB (cloud) to 512 KB (TinyML), a 268 million-fold reduction. Power runs from about 3 MW for the cloud pod to the TinyML tier’s average-power target of < 1 mW. Hardware cost spans six orders of magnitude, from multimillion-dollar clusters to $10 microcontrollers. Together with facility overheads such as power usage effectiveness (PUE),11 these spreads decide which paradigm can serve a workload economically, and they explain why model compression becomes essential as the budget tightens.

10 ML hardware cost spectrum: The million-fold cost range makes deployment paradigm selection an economics decision as well as a physics decision. The same accuracy target may be achievable on a low-cost microcontroller only after substantial model reduction, or on an expensive data-center accelerator with a much larger resource budget, and the two designs carry different latency, power, and operational cost profiles.

11 Power usage effectiveness (PUE): Total facility energy divided by IT equipment energy over the same period (The Green Grid 2007), developed in Power and Energy Measurement. It captures facility overhead, including cooling and power distribution. Even a PUE of 1.06 leaves substantial noncompute power at data-center scale. TinyML does not require dedicated facility cooling, although sensing, communication, conversion losses, and sleep power still contribute to its complete system-energy budget. These device-level costs sit outside PUE.

The Green Grid. 2007. Green Grid Data Center Power Efficiency Metrics: PUE and DCIE. The Green Grid.

The hardware spectrum turns the bottleneck diagnosis into a fit test, in which each workload must be matched to the tier whose compute, bandwidth, power, and cost envelope it can satisfy. These platforms are products of decades of hardware evolution, from floating-point coprocessors in the 1980s through graphics processors in the 2000s to today’s domain-specific AI accelerators.

↪ The progression from floating-point coprocessors to domain-specific AI accelerators is developed in Specialized computing.

Systems Perspective 2.1: System balance across paradigms
The last column of table 4 reads each tier through the pipelined bound in equation 5. For cloud and edge the binding constraint is a term of that bound, whereas for mobile and TinyML it is a limit the time equation leaves out, and in every tier the dominant constraint sets the optimization strategy. Compute-bound cloud training rewards higher accelerator utilization and larger batches. Bandwidth-bound large language model (LLM) decode (token-by-token generation) and bandwidth-bound edge inference reward moving fewer bytes—through smaller models, lower precision, and more reuse per loaded weight—so a faster accelerator helps them little. Moving to battery-powered devices adds an implicit energy term. Mobile systems integrate power over time under a tight thermal cap, which makes lower precision and duty cycling pay off in both battery life and sustained speed. TinyML faces a strict memory precondition before any time or energy term applies, because weights and code must fit in flash and activations and runtime state must fit in SRAM through compact models and low-precision arithmetic. Acting on the binding term maps each dominant term to the optimizations that work and the ones that are wasted, turning this diagnosis into an action plan.

Each paradigm is therefore an operating envelope with different resource budgets, and moving from warehouse data centers down to microcontrollers traces a physical gradient that trades raw compute throughput for energy and memory efficiency. The four sections that follow walk that gradient one tier at a time. Each asks the same four questions, which the constraint map in each section draws as four branches: which constraint binds, how the system responds, where the paradigm fails, and which workloads fit. Because that structure is shared, each section concentrates on what is distinctive about its tier and on the one or two calculations that decide whether a workload belongs there.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why is single-image (batch=1) ResNet-50 inference memory-bound on both a high-end cloud accelerator (NVIDIA A100) and a mobile NPU (iPhone 15 Pro)?

    1. ResNet-50 contains dynamic control flow that causes branching stalls on SIMD execution units.
    2. Both platforms lack matrix multiplication coprocessors, forcing scalar CPU execution.
    3. At batch=1, model weights must be loaded from memory once per single image without weight reuse across batch items, keeping arithmetic intensity below each platform’s hardware balance point (\(R_{\text{peak}}/\text{BW}\)).
    4. The activations at batch=1 exceed the total high-bandwidth memory (HBM) and SRAM capacities of both devices.

  2. Why does a convolutional neural network (e.g., ResNet-50) shift from a compute-bound regime during cloud training to a memory-bound regime during single-sample (\(\text{batch}=1\)) edge inference?

  3. An ML service SLA guarantees a total end-to-end response time of 100 ms. The pipeline involves client preprocessing, a cross-region network RPC, model inference, and postprocessing. According to critical path latency principles, why will this system fail if a cross-region network call taking 120 ms is placed on the synchronous critical path?

    1. The ML framework compiler will refuse to lower the graph if network RPC operations are detected.
    2. Cross-region RPCs corrupt the numerical precision of FP16 tensors during serialization.
    3. A cross-region network call reduces the accelerator compute throughput (\(R_{\text{peak}}\)) by half.
    4. The critical path is the longest sequential chain of dependent operations; any single stage exceeding 100 ms guarantees the end-to-end SLA will be breached regardless of how fast all other stages execute.

  4. What is the hardware balance point (or roofline ridge point) of an accelerator, and what does it tell an ML systems engineer about whether a workload will be compute-bound or memory-bound?

  5. For batch-1 LLM decode in the cloud and for most edge inference, the iron-law term that binds first is memory ____ rather than peak compute, so a faster accelerator alone yields little speedup.

  6. Order the four architectural layers of the single-node stack from the top-level mission goal down to the physical execution engine:

  1. Operating system (resource orchestration and host-accelerator memory transfers)
  2. ML framework (operator graph lowering and execution planning)
  3. Hardware and silicon contract (peak compute throughput, memory bandwidth, HBM/SRAM)
  4. Application layer (training throughput target or inference latency SLA)

See Answers →

2.5 Cloud ML: Computational Power

At the top of that physical gradient sits Cloud ML, which pools power, cooling, and accelerators in centralized data centers to serve workloads that no local machine can hold. The estimated resources needed to train GPT-3 (Brown et al. 2020) show why this tier exists: 3,634 PFLOP-days of computation, equivalent to 10,000 V100 GPUs running for approximately 15 days at the reported sustained rate, and about 1.3 GWh of electricity (Patterson et al. 2021). A PFLOP-day is \(10^{15}\) FLOP/s sustained for one day, about \(8.6 \times 10^{19}\) FLOPs, so this total is the same few hundred zettaFLOPs estimated from the model’s parameter and token counts in The deep learning era and the infrastructure bottleneck. At the hardware rates used in that estimate, a smartphone or edge server could not have completed this training run on a practical schedule; it required a data-center-scale cluster with enough aggregate compute, memory, and storage. The bitter lesson shows up here as hardware: general methods that improve with computation keep winning, and the computation they consume concentrates wherever megawatts of power and liquid cooling can be pooled. When network latency can be tolerated, the cloud offers computational scale that no other paradigm can match, although a model trained in the cloud or a smaller derived version can later be served near users and sensors.

Brown, Tom B., Benjamin Mann, Nick Ryder, Melanie Subbiah, Jared Kaplan, Prafulla Dhariwal, Arvind Neelakantan, et al. 2020. “Language Models Are Few-Shot Learners.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 1877–901. https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2005.14165.
Patterson, David, Joseph Gonzalez, Quoc Le, Chen Liang, Lluis-Miquel Munguia, Daniel Rothchild, David So, Maud Texier, and Jeff Dean. 2021. “Carbon Emissions and Large Neural Network Training.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2104.10350.

↩︎ The bitter lesson, the book’s account of why compute-scaled general methods keep winning, is established in The Bitter Lesson.

12 Cloud as utility computing: A provisioning model in which a provider owns and operates a specialized hardware portfolio that few organizations could economically maintain themselves and rents it on demand. Renting gives direct access to the architecture each workload archetype requires, such as dense accelerator pods for compute-bound workloads, HBM-equipped nodes for bandwidth-bound workloads, and high-memory systems with fast interconnects for capacity-bound workloads, without purchasing and maintaining the entire installation.

Centralizing these resources under a utility model12 allows providers to assemble specialized accelerator topologies tailored to distinct hardware demands. This infrastructure serves as the natural home for three of the four workload archetypes. Compute-bound workloads like ResNet training demand sustained TFLOP/s across thousands of accelerators. Bandwidth-bound workloads like large language model inference benefit from TB/s HBM bandwidth. Capacity-bound workloads like DLRM-style recommendation engines have embedding tables that routinely exceed terabytes in aggregate, far beyond any single accelerator. Those tables are sharded across pooled host and accelerator memory, so distributed lookups and training updates place heavy demands on the cluster interconnect. Voice assistants show the split between tiers: they lean on the cloud for language understanding while their always-on wake-word stage runs on the device, a division that section 2.10 quantifies. Even as cloud deployments scale from single-accelerator instances to warehouse-scale clusters, systems engineering at every one of these scales begins on a single node, with memory bus saturation, PCIe and NVLink bandwidth, and accelerator utilization inside one machine.

↪ The memory-capacity bounds and sparse gather patterns of high-cardinality embedding tables are developed in Sparse Architectures: Recommendation Systems.

Definition 2.1: Cloud ML

Cloud machine learning is the deployment paradigm that trades latency for elastic compute by locating ML workloads in centralized data centers, decoupling computational capacity from the physical location of data sources and users.

  1. Significance: Cloud deployment dominates the \(\PeakRate\) term: a cloud region can pool thousands of accelerators, with access governed by capacity, quotas, and cost. The trade-off is the \(\Latency\) term: a minimum round-trip latency of 10–100 ms (set by the speed of light over continental distances) makes those distant cloud paths infeasible for workloads requiring sub-10 ms response.
  2. Distinction: Unlike edge ML, which prioritizes local response and data locality at fixed \(\PeakRate\), cloud ML prioritizes elastic \(\PeakRate\) at the cost of variable \(\Latency\).
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that cloud ML is “unlimited compute.” In reality, the distance penalty \((\Latency)\) and the ingestion bottleneck \((\DataVolume/\Bandwidth)\) are physics constraints that no software optimization can eliminate, setting a hard floor on response time for any workload whose data originates outside the data center.

Pooling elastic accelerators and sharding models overcome local capacity ceilings, but they meet hard failure boundaries in speed-of-light propagation latency and internet dependence (figure 4). Workloads that thrive in this regime (such as asynchronous fraud detection, batch recommendation updates, and large language model pretraining) accept this trade because scale matters more than instantaneous response. When an application requires real-time control, however, network latency stops being an engineering inconvenience and becomes a hard physical barrier. The light-barrier calculation already ruled out a cross-country data center for a 10 ms budget; the sharper question is whether a regional data center, much closer to the machine it protects, fares any better.

Napkin Math 2.2: The distance penalty
Problem: Consider a real-time safety monitor for a robotic arm on a factory floor. The safety logic requires a 10 ms end-to-end response time to prevent injury, so the distance penalty imposed by the light barrier matters. The model runs in a regional cloud data center 1,500 km away, less than half the cross-country distance. Can the safety budget be met?

Physics:

  1. Light in fiber: ~200,000 km/s.
  2. Round-trip propagation: (1,500 km \(\times\) 2)/200,000 km/s = 15 ms.
  3. Result: Round-trip propagation alone requires 15 ms, exceeding the 10 ms end-to-end budget by 5 ms before the model performs any inference.

Systems insight: The specified regional cloud path misses the budget on propagation alone and is therefore infeasible. Running the model beside the arm removes that remote propagation term, but the complete local sensing, inference, and control path must still be checked against the deadline.

2.5.1 Cloud infrastructure and scale

When a workload does not sit inside a millisecond control loop, however, accepting that network distance buys physical scale inside the data center that no local facility can approach. Training a frontier model across hundreds of terabytes of data requires connecting thousands of accelerator chips so tightly that they behave like one computer. TPU supercomputer designs organize thousands of specialized accelerator chips into data-center-scale systems that deliver PFLOP/s-to-EFLOP/s reduced-precision throughput (Jouppi et al. 2023), orders of magnitude beyond the edge and mobile rows of table 4. Warehouse-scale facilities pack aisles of liquid-cooled racks and dense optical cabling that draw megawatts to sustain that exaFLOP-scale matrix throughput (figure 5), providing the physical scale that makes training on hundreds of terabytes of data practical.

↪ What changes when training extends beyond a single node is developed in Scaling beyond a single node.

Centralization also changes how models are served and governed. Cloud APIs make trained models accessible worldwide across mobile, web, and IoT platforms. Shared infrastructure enables engineering teams to collaborate with unified version control, while pay-as-you-go pricing models convert upfront capital expenditure (CapEx) into usage-based operational expenditure (OpEx) that scales with demand.

Scale does not make the cloud universally superior. The data-gravity principle (2) explains why. Shipping a dataset of size \(\DataVolume\) over a network link of bandwidth \(\NetworkBandwidth\) takes \(\DataVolume/\NetworkBandwidth\), a time that grows with the data, while shipping the computation (a model and its code) costs roughly the same however much data waits at the other end. As data volume grows, moving the data to a remote cluster therefore eventually costs more than moving the computation to the data. Data gravity works through the transfer arithmetic, and the edge section applies the same comparison to a single request under a deadline. The deployment paradigm is dictated by the binding physical constraint, not by the largest available throughput.

2.5.2 Cloud ML trade-offs and constraints

A regional cloud outage can take down every connected client at once, and a sudden traffic spike can turn an elastic GPU pool into a runaway monthly bill. For latency-sensitive workloads, the distance penalty is the cloud’s most consequential cost, and network jitter makes it unpredictable as well as large. Three further costs follow from centralization: the exposure of data the cloud must receive, spending that scales with use, and dependence on the network.

The first cost is privacy and security. Transmitting sensitive data to remote data centers expands the disclosure surface and complicates regulatory compliance. Organizations handling data subject to regulations such as the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) or the Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA) must select, document, and maintain safeguards appropriate to the data, system, and applicable legal duties. Privacy-preserving approaches can reduce how much sensitive data leaves its original environment, but they complement rather than replace infrastructure controls.

The second cost is financial, because cloud infrastructure turns upfront hardware purchases into ongoing operating bills. Comparing paradigms by raw accelerator hourly rates obscures the cumulative expenses of network egress, persistent storage, and managed orchestration. Sound evaluation instead compares total cost of ownership (TCO), which includes power, cooling, and labor and weighs upfront capital expense against recurring operating expense over the system’s lifetime.

Consider a vision service that runs 1M daily inferences at ResNet-50 scale (10 ms latency, 100 KB response) over a 3 years horizon. In the cloud configuration, 4 instances are provisioned for redundancy and peak-traffic headroom rather than average throughput alone. Table 5 itemizes the annual GPU, network, load-balancer, and observability costs of that fixed-capacity cloud deployment under public list pricing, and table 6 itemizes the hardware, power, cooling, network, and DevOps labor costs of an on-premises edge server. Both tables bound an illustrative partial budget rather than a complete, matched TCO, because cloud operating labor, edge redundancy, site installation, and failure recovery are omitted and equivalent availability has not been established.

Napkin Math 2.3: Cloud vs. edge annual budgets
Problem: Under the partial accounting boundaries itemized in table 5 and table 6, which implementation has the lower estimated annual budget over 3 years?

Math: The question is the annual cost at this operating point, so the comparison is the gap between the cloud and edge annual totals.

At 1M inferences/day, the gap is ~$35,288/year − ~$21,610/year = ~$13,678/year. Almost all of the cloud cost is provisioned capacity rather than use. At 10 ms per request the day’s traffic needs only about 2.8 GPU-hours of the 96 provisioned, about 2.9 percent utilization. Only egress scales with traffic.

Result: The listed edge budget costs about 38.8 percent less at this operating point. Because the fixed cloud pool alone already costs more than the listed edge budget, no traffic level under this fixed provisioning and partial accounting makes the cloud cheaper. A genuine break-even analysis would need cloud capacity that scales with demand.

Systems insight: The listed edge budget is dominated by labor (~$15,000 ÷ ~$21,610/year ≈ 69.4 percent), not hardware. Organizations without existing DevOps capacity should factor in the full cost of maintaining on-premises infrastructure.

Unpredictable usage spikes also complicate budgeting and call for automated monitoring and cost governance. Network dependency, the last of the three costs, creates an availability hazard: any connectivity disruption between clients and the data center immediately degrades or halts service. Proprietary cloud APIs compound the dependency with vendor lock-in, making it harder to move workloads from one provider to another. Even so, centralized infrastructure remains uniquely suited to consumer-scale services that require elastic capacity, such as voice assistants and recommendation engines. These applications trade latency for scale, accepting remote-path delay in exchange for computational resources that cannot be supplied locally. The trade fails when a latency budget drops below what the remote path permits or when data volumes exceed what networks can carry, and computation must then move closer to the data source.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In the chapter’s 3-year budget comparison for an on-premises edge server deployment serving 1 million inferences per day, what is the largest component of the edge system’s listed annual budget in the chapter’s scenario?

    1. GPU hardware silicon purchase price amortized over 3 years
    2. DevOps engineering labor required to maintain, update, and manage on-premises edge infrastructure
    3. Electricity costs for running the server 24/7
    4. Dedicated server room air-conditioning and cooling overhead

  2. What is the data-gravity principle in cloud ML, and why does it make moving multi-terabyte raw sensor datasets to the cloud economically and physically problematic?

  3. Under GDPR Article 17 (Right to Erasure), when a user requests deletion of their data, an organization is legally mandated to retrain from scratch every ML model that ever encountered that user’s training samples.

2.6 Edge ML: Latency and Privacy

The robotic-arm safety monitor in napkin math 2.2, an industrial IoT (IIoT) control loop,13 left one option open: running the model beside the arm it protects. Edge ML is built on that move, trading elastic cloud capacity for shorter request paths and local data retention.

13 Industrial IoT (IIoT): A domain where latency constraints can be set by physical safety rather than user perception. Keeping sensing and the stop decision local removes the long-haul network term but leaves the edge system bounded by its local compute capacity \((\PeakRate)\).

In terms of the pipelined iron law (equation 5), placing computation adjacent to the sensor removes the wide-area offload term \(\IoTransferTime\) and the remote propagation floor from the request path, leaving execution time bounded by local compute and memory transfer. Proximity alone does not guarantee a sub-100 ms response, however, because the local hardware has fixed capacity and the complete sensing-to-actuation path must still clear the deadline.

Definition 2.2: Edge ML

Edge machine learning is the deployment paradigm optimized for local response and data locality by locating computation physically adjacent to data sources.

  1. Significance: It circumvents the distance penalty \((\Latency)\) of the cloud, trading elastic scale for a fixed local compute capacity \((\PeakRate)\).
  2. Distinction: Unlike cloud ML, which prioritizes throughput, edge ML prioritizes response deadlines and local data control. Unlike TinyML, edge ML may still use workstation-class accelerators such as general-purpose GPUs (GPGPUs).
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that edge ML refers to a specific hardware class. In reality, it is a location paradigm: it spans from IoT gateways to on-premises servers, unified by physical proximity to the data source.

Real-time deadlines are not the only driver. Data sovereignty and privacy rules can restrict transmitting raw sensor streams across external networks, which places computation on local gateways, industrial PCs, and on-premises servers. Placing compute near sensors and filtering data locally helps meet sub-100 ms response budgets, while introducing failure boundaries in fixed node capacity and remote fleet management (figure 6).

Raw edge data can be wider than the network pipe.

14 Internet of things (IoT) data wall: IoT deployments depend on continuous sensor streams from devices distributed across homes, factories, farms, vehicles, and infrastructure (McKinsey Global Institute 2021). In many of those settings, the aggregate \(\DataVolume\) from raw streams overwhelms the available uplink budget or latency budget for centralized ingestion, making local edge processing an architectural requirement rather than merely a cost optimization.

McKinsey Global Institute. 2021. The Internet of Things: Catching up to an Accelerating Opportunity. McKinsey & Company.

Even when latency budgets are relaxed, the second binding constraint in figure 6, where raw sensor data is wider than the network pipe, can rule out cloud ingestion on bandwidth alone. When continuous IoT sensor ingestion rates14 exceed uplink capacity, local filtering or inference must shrink the transmitted stream before it reaches the wide-area link, and the complete local and remote paths then determine where inference can meet the application’s requirements.

Napkin Math 2.4: The bandwidth bottleneck
Problem: Consider a quality control system for a factory floor with 100 cameras running at 30 FPS with 1080p resolution. Does video streaming become the bandwidth bottleneck, and does edge ML reduce the bandwidth enough to process locally?

Physics:

  1. Raw data rate per camera: 1920 \(\times\) 1080 \(\times\) 3 bytes \(\times\) 30 FPS ≈ 186.6 MB/s.
  2. Total data rate: 100 cameras \(\times\) 186.6 MB/s = 18.7 GB/s.
  3. Cloud transfer exposure: Uploading raw camera feeds is primarily a bandwidth, ingest, storage, and processing problem; per-GB cloud egress charges apply when data is transferred back out of the cloud. If the raw stream were later retrieved at $0.09/GB data-transfer-out pricing, the transfer charge alone would reach $4.4M/month.
  4. Network reality: Even a dedicated 10 Gbps line (1.25 GB/s) cannot carry the load, because the workload demands 14.9× more bandwidth than exists.

Systems insight: Streaming these raw feeds over the stated link is impossible. Local processing or a smaller transmitted representation is required; compression changes the bandwidth calculation but must preserve the information and timing needed for defect detection. If an edge server transmits only defect metadata (1 KB per detection at roughly 20 events/s across the floor), bandwidth falls by about 933,120×.

Uncompressed sensor streams can saturate an uplink, which is the bandwidth barrier to centralized ingestion. For battery-powered edge devices (wireless cameras, drones, wearables), the energy cost of wireless communication introduces an even stricter physical ceiling. Offloading adds energy to the time-based analysis of equation 5, so the local-versus-offload decision must weigh joules as well as milliseconds.

Napkin Math 2.5: The energy of transmission
Problem: Should a battery-powered sensor process data locally (on-device NPU inference) or send it to the cloud when the energy of transmission must come out of a fixed battery budget?

Given:

  • Data \((\DataVolume)\): 1 MB (illustrative payload volume).
  • Transmission energy \((\TransmissionEnergyPerByte)\): 100 mJ/MB (Wi-Fi/LTE).
  • Compute energy \((\LocalInferenceEnergy)\): 0.1 mJ/inference (illustrative MobileNetV2 anchor for the neural processing unit alone, excluding the rest of the phone).

Math:

  1. Cloud approach: \(\CloudOffloadEnergy\approx \DataVolume \times \TransmissionEnergyPerByte\) = 1 MB \(\times\) 100 mJ/MB = 100 mJ.
  2. Local approach: \(\LocalInferenceEnergy\approx\) Inference = 0.1 mJ.

Systems insight: Transmitting raw data is 1,000× more expensive than processing it locally. Charging local inference with the whole phone’s energy over the inference window (2.6 mJ, the MobileNetV2 bar charted in figure 7) still leaves transmission 39× more expensive. Even if the cloud had infinite speed \((\Time\approx 0)\), the stated battery budget favors local inference over raw-data transmission. The machine constraint (battery) shapes the deployment choice.

Figure 7 steps back from that matched comparison. It supplies energy scale anchors for different tasks and accounting boundaries on a logarithmic scale spanning eight orders of magnitude. The figure isolates the 100,000,000× gap between an always-on TinyML keyword spotting inference (about 10 µJ) and a cloud foundation-model query (about 1 kJ, an order-of-magnitude scenario anchor that includes data-center overhead rather than a measured value).

Systems Perspective 2.2: Reading the energy-per-inference numbers
The logarithmic comparisons plotted in figure 7 combine solver-derived device-energy estimates with separate scale anchors; they are not controlled full-system measurements. Each vision bar counts one device over its batch-1 inference window. The cloud ResNet-50 bar, for example, charges an A100 averaging about ~130 W for 0.57 ms. It excludes the host CPUs, memory, networking, and cooling that bring an entire server to about ~1 kW, so a whole-server accounting of the same request would be several times larger.

The span becomes tangible when measured in a familiar unit of energy, one smartphone battery. The nominal energy stored in a 3.7 V, 3000 mAh battery is equivalent to about 4 billion inferences by the keyword spotter but only about 40 queries to a cloud LLM. The comparison is one of scale only: a cloud query’s energy is spent in the data center, not drawn from the phone. Nor are the keyword-spotting counts elapsed battery life: actual runtime still depends on inference cadence, sleep power, sensing, communication, and the rest of the device.

2.6.1 The data locality invariant: When the network is too slow

Even if a battery-powered drone or camera could afford the radio energy to stream raw video, the wireless link would still fail a more immediate test: time.
