When a delivery drone captures 4K video at 60 frames per second to dodge obstacles, shipping even one uncompressed frame over a cellular link takes longer than the 16.7 ms interval before the next frame arrives. That per-request bandwidth-latency trade-off governs the choice between local edge processing and remote cloud offload. The data locality invariant below is the per-request, deadline-bound form of the data-gravity principle from the cloud section. Where data gravity weighs moving a whole dataset against moving the computation, the invariant weighs moving one input against one deadline. A remote path is rejected when its complete time exceeds the service deadline, and among the paths that remain, the faster one wins.

Definition 2.3: The data locality invariant

The data locality invariant first screens a remote path against the service deadline. The expression below gives a lower bound on complete remote-path time \(\RemotePathTime\); let \(\CompletionDeadline\) denote the deadline, \(\NetworkBandwidth\) offload bandwidth, \(\NetworkLatency\) network latency, and \(\RemotePeakRate\) and \(\RemoteHardwareEfficiency\) the remote compute rate and efficiency: \[\RemotePathTime \ge \frac{\DataVolume}{\NetworkBandwidth} + \NetworkLatency + \frac{\Operations}{\RemotePeakRate \cdot \RemoteHardwareEfficiency}\]

The bound includes input transmission, network round-trip time, and arithmetic execution, but omits remote memory movement, queueing, preprocessing, runtime overhead, and response serialization. If even this lower bound exceeds the deadline, reject the remote path. A bound below the deadline leaves a candidate for complete-path measurement; it does not establish feasibility. Compare complete local and remote times only after each path meets the deadline and its resource constraints. Here, \(\NetworkLatency\) covers propagation, routing, and protocol handshakes; response serialization may be small for a class label but must be included when material.

  1. Significance: The invariant defines a crossover beyond which adding remote compute \((\RemotePeakRate)\) has little effect because transmission dominates the lower bound. The local path is preferred only after its own complete execution time and resource limits are checked.
  2. Distinction: Unlike the iron law, which decomposes one execution path, the locality invariant uses a deadline to reject an idealized remote path, then requires complete-path evidence to choose between the remaining alternatives.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that 5G/6G “solves” locality. While these technologies improve \(\NetworkBandwidth\), they do not remove propagation and protocol latency, so a remote path can still miss a tight service deadline.

The locality formulation also explains what every local tier gains, edge, mobile, and TinyML alike. Local execution removes network transit time (\(\DataVolume/\NetworkBandwidth + \NetworkLatency\)) from the request path, eliminating wide-area network variability. A closed-loop latency guarantee requires validating a bound on the complete local path under its operating conditions, \(\LocalPathTime\le \CompletionDeadline\). Uplink traffic drops from raw sensor bitrates to lightweight event telemetry, relieving congestion and recurring transit charges. Keeping raw sensor data where it is captured shrinks the transmission attack surface, although local processing alone does not guarantee privacy: data at rest still needs access control, and retention and secondary use still need governance. Local inference also continues through wide-area outages whenever local power and sensor links remain functional. Mobile and TinyML inherit all four gains; what distinguishes them is what each gives up to obtain them. A single high-rate sensor frame under a strict frame deadline is enough to force that crossover.

Napkin Math 2.6: The locality crossover
Problem: Should a drone’s object avoidance system (4K, 60 FPS) offload to the cloud, or does this become a locality crossover?

Given:

  • Data \((\DataVolume)\): 4K frame ≈ 24.9 MB.
  • Bandwidth \((\NetworkBandwidth)\): 100 Mb/s cellular uplink.
  • Remote network and compute response: 110 ms (round-trip + remote compute).
  • Per-frame completion deadline: 16.7 ms, equal to the frame interval in this illustrative scenario; each avoidance result must be ready before the next frame arrives.

Math:

  1. Transmission time: 24.9 MB \(\times\) 8 bits/100 Mb/s = 1,990.7 ms.
  2. Remote-path lower bound: Transmission + 110 ms remote response = 2100.7 ms, vs. the stated 16.7 ms per-frame deadline.

Systems insight: The remote path misses the stated per-frame deadline, and faster remote compute cannot remove the transmission time. Cloud offload is therefore infeasible under these assumptions; the local path must still be measured to confirm that it meets the deadline.

2.6.2 Edge ML deployment challenges

Keeping inference on the factory floor or inside a retail store eliminates the wide-area network delay, but now the model must fit inside whatever compute box is bolted to the wall. If a firmware update fails across a thousand stores, no data-center operations team can walk down the aisle to swap the board. Edge deployment trades network transit for fixed local capacity. Edge hardware spans a wide power envelope, from 5–50 W embedded modules to site-powered servers drawing hundreds of watts, and its binding limit is the fixed capacity of a site rather than the elastic capacity of a cloud region. Many smaller embedded modules operate within 1–8 GB of memory,15 and their power and board budgets cannot support the wide, high-power interfaces of HBM. They rely on LPDDR or shared system DRAM instead, so memory capacity limits parameter scale and memory bandwidth limits throughput. Fitting modern machine learning models into these bounds requires efficiency techniques that store weights in fewer bits (quantization), remove weights that contribute little (pruning), or train a smaller model to imitate a larger one (distillation). Model footprint, activation buffers, runtime state, and thermal duty cycles all become binding. Operationally, coordinating software updates and model versions across thousands of heterogeneous, physically exposed devices requires specialized deployment pipelines,16 where intermittent links and physical access complicate rollback and failure diagnosis.

15 Embedded edge constraints: Across representative edge envelopes, 1–8 GB can hold roughly 1–8 billion INT8 weights before activations, runtime state, and other buffers, so the memory budget, not a fixed parameter count, sets the ceiling. Sustainable compute is further limited by power, cooling, and duty cycle.

16 Edge fleet coordination: Managing thousands of distributed edge devices amplifies coordination problems: intermittent connectivity causes model version drift, hardware heterogeneity requires per-target optimization, and limited remote access can make failed firmware rollbacks require a site visit.

↪ Over-the-air updates, dual-bank rollback, and lightweight fleet telemetry for edge devices are developed in Edge AI deployment.

A realistic retail deployment shows how those constraints turn into throughput, hardware, and fleet-cost requirements. Consider a smart retail chain that deploys person detection across 500 stores, each with 20 cameras/store running at 15 FPS. Table 7 cascades the per-store inference rate through the per-frame FLOP count of YOLOv8-nano, a compact object-detection model, to yield the throughput each store must sustain. The 2\(\times\) headroom keeps the device out of saturation when traffic bursts above the average rate. When the model is quantized to 8-bit integers for edge execution, each floating-point operation maps to one integer operation, converting the floating-point rate directly into an equivalent INT8 TOPS requirement.

Three candidate edge accelerators, including an embedded GPU accelerator, differ sharply in the power, unit cost, and fleet cost of reaching that throughput target (table 8).

Napkin Math 2.7: Edge inference sizing
Problem: Given the throughput target in table 7 and the candidates in table 8, which edge accelerator (USB-scale TPU, workstation-class embedded GPU, or general-purpose mini-PC) delivers the required throughput at the lowest estimated three-year hardware-and-power budget?

Math: The capacity screen uses an assumed sustained rate rather than the datasheet peak. The scenario’s 50 percent derating plays the role of the efficiency \(\EfficiencyHw\) in the iron law. One Jetson derates from 100 TOPS to 50 TOPS, far above the 5.22 TOPS requirement. One NUC derates to 0.5 TOPS and falls short; meeting the target would take 11 per store, about $2,200,000 of hardware across the fleet. A Coral Dev Board is rated at 4 TOPS peak. The same derating gives 2 TOPS, so the count per store is 5.22 TOPS required ÷ 2 TOPS per board = 2.6 boards, which rounds up to 3 boards/store. This estimate assumes the model’s operations and numerical format are supported and that work can be divided across the boards at the assumed rate.

Over 3 years across 500 stores, the hardware-and-power estimate for that configuration is Hardware $225,000 + Power (0.006 kW \(\times\) 500 \(\times\) 8760 h/year \(\times\) 3 years \(\times\) $0.12/kWh = $9,461) = $234,461 total. For comparison, a baseline of one dedicated cloud GPU per store, at the same $0.75/hr instance rate as the cloud-versus-edge comparison, costs ~$9,855,000. Within this accounting boundary, the other two candidates need no power arithmetic to rank, because the Coral configuration’s hardware-and-power estimate is already below the Jetson fleet’s $300,000 hardware cost and far below a feasible NUC fleet’s. Integration, maintenance, redundancy, and matched cloud operating costs remain outside this comparison.

Result: One Coral alone is undersized under these assumptions, but the sharded configuration with 3 boards/store is a low-cost edge candidate within the hardware-and-power boundary, with an estimated 6 TOPS and about 1.3× lower per-store hardware CapEx than Jetson. Jetson remains the simpler single-device candidate when integration complexity matters more than hardware cost.

Systems insight: Edge sizing is a capacity-and-cost problem, not a device-name problem. The cheapest feasible design may be several small accelerators per site rather than one larger board, but that hardware saving must be weighed against the coordination and maintenance burden of a sharded edge fleet. The estimate narrows the candidates; measurements of the actual model and runtime must establish task quality, throughput, and end-to-end latency before deployment.

Edge hardware is also physically exposed. Unlike servers housed in access-controlled data centers, edge hardware deployed across retail floors or public infrastructure is physically accessible to adversaries. This exposure requires hardware-enforced protection mechanisms: secure boot chains to prevent unauthorized firmware modification, encrypted local storage for model weights and intermediate activations, and tamper-evident enclosures. Capital expenditures also scale linearly with deployment footprint, so equipping 1,000 sites with $500–2,000 of edge hardware per site requires $500,000–2,000,000 in upfront CapEx before installation, cabling, and fleet operations. Whether that upfront investment yields a lower total cost of ownership than recurring cloud inference depends on utilization duty cycles, local energy rates, and site maintenance overhead.

2.6.3 Real-time industrial and IoT systems

Organizations accept that upfront capital cost and fleet-management burden only when physical locality makes remote execution impossible. Autonomous vehicles show the strictest latency case: safety-critical perception and control decisions must fit millisecond-scale budgets as the vehicle moves. Onboard systems process streams from multiple cameras and other sensors through local hardware because a remote cloud path cannot satisfy the control loop when network delay alone exceeds the deadline.

In smart retail, the per-store camera sizing in napkin math 2.7 extends from person detection to full store state, where uplink bandwidth and privacy bind before compute does. A checkout-free store17 processes feeds from many cameras on local edge servers, tracking customer movements and item selections without continuously transmitting raw video over a wide-area link. This architecture reduces bandwidth demand and transmission exposure, but it does not by itself ensure privacy: retention, access, secondary use, and governance controls still determine the system’s compliance and risk.

17 Checkout-free retail: A store design that fuses camera observations with shelf events and transaction state locally, then transmits only selected events or aggregates. Those derived records still fall inside the privacy boundary.

18 Industry 4.0: The fourth industrial revolution integrates ML into the sensor-actuator feedback loop on factory floors. In an illustrative welding-robot scenario, defect detection samples at 60 Hz, giving a 16.7 ms sampling interval; if each response must finish before the next sample, the complete sensing-to-actuation path must fit that deadline. This completion requirement is a scenario assumption; the sampling rate alone does not establish the control deadline or guarantee safe operation.

19 Predictive maintenance: The use of sensor data such as vibration and temperature to estimate equipment condition and failure risk. Continuous monitoring makes sensing cadence, duty cycle, power, and local storage first-class constraints. Battery-powered monitoring nodes may require sub-watt average operation, whereas mains-powered industrial gateways can use a much larger envelope.

On the factory floor, the industrial IoT (often called Industry 4.0 when ML closes the factory control loop18) combines the latency constraint of the robotic-arm monitor (napkin math 2.2) with the uplink wall of multi-camera inspection (napkin math 2.4). Manufacturing facilities deploy these local loops where millisecond-level responsiveness governs real-time quality control and where continuous vibration and thermal sensing drive predictive maintenance.19

Many industrial and retail edge systems are stationary and connected to site power, where a factory edge server drawing hundreds of watts around the clock remains viable. Mobile devices, by contrast, travel with untethered users on strictly bounded battery reserves. When computation moves from stationary infrastructure to an untethered mobile device, battery energy becomes a central constraint alongside latency, and the dominant optimization target shifts from raw execution time to energy per inference.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A manufacturing facility deploys 100 cameras recording uncompressed 1080p video (\(1920 \times 1080 \times 3\text{ bytes/pixel}\)) at 30 FPS. The factory has a dedicated 10 Gbps (~1.25 GB/s) fiber uplink. Why is streaming all raw video to a cloud ML server physically impossible?

    1. The total is only about \(186.6\text{ MB/s}\) because that is the per-camera rate, so the 10 Gbps uplink has ample headroom.
    2. The aggregate is about \(18.7\text{ Gb/s}\) because the camera output should be compared numerically to 10 Gbps without converting bytes to bits, so the link is close enough to be practical.
    3. The aggregate raw video data rate is approximately \(18.7\text{ GB/s}\), which exceeds the \(1.25\text{ GB/s}\) uplink capacity by roughly \(15\times\).
    4. The aggregate raw stream is large, but normal compression assumptions would make it fit within the 10 Gbps uplink in practice.

  2. An autonomous drone navigates at 60 FPS (16.7 ms per-frame budget) capturing uncompressed 4K frames (~24.9 MB per frame). The drone connects via a 100 Mbps uplink to a cloud server whose round-trip latency plus inference time is 110 ms. Applying the data locality invariant, what is the feasibility of offloading this obstacle avoidance system to the cloud?

    1. Feasible, because the cloud GPU computes inference in under 10 ms.
    2. Feasible, provided 5G network slicing is enabled on the drone modem.
    3. Infeasible, solely because cloud servers do not support drone telemetry protocols.
    4. Infeasible, because transmitting a 24.9 MB frame over a 100 Mbps link requires ~2,000 ms, causing even the remote-path lower-bound estimate (~2,100 ms) to violate the 16.7 ms frame deadline by over two orders of magnitude.

  3. Why cannot high-bandwidth wireless networking technologies (such as 5G or 6G) eliminate the need for edge ML in tight real-time control loops (\(<10\text{ ms}\))?

  4. A retail store needs about 5.2 TOPS of sustained throughput. Why does the chapter’s estimate call for three 4 TOPS boards rather than two, even though two boards’ combined 8 TOPS peak exceeds the target?

  5. An illustrative 60 Hz industrial control scenario assumes each sensing-to-actuation response must finish before the next sample. Its complete ML control loop latency (\(L_{\text{lat}}\)) must therefore fit within one ____ (about 16.7 ms).

See Answers →

2.7 Mobile ML: Offline Intelligence

Carrying edge-style locality into a user’s pocket changes which budget binds. A phone must deliver responsive, private inference all day from a pocket-sized battery, inside a thin chassis with no fan to carry heat away. Mobile ML integrates machine learning directly into smartphones and tablets to meet that envelope. It fits workloads where data locality, offline operation, and immediate response matter more than computational scale, such as voice recognition, computational photography,20 health monitoring, and navigation or translation that must keep working without a network. Its battery and thermal envelope suit frequent, short-duration inference rather than sustained heavy computation.

20 Computational photography: The use of ML algorithms (for example, multi-frame fusion and neural denoising) to overcome the physical limits of small mobile camera sensors.

21 Mobile vision model reduction: MobileNet-style architectures reduce the computation in common vision layers while preserving useful accuracy for mobile tasks. The systems point here is that mobile deployment often requires changing the model family, not merely moving the same model to a phone.

Where stationary cloud and edge deployments optimize latency \(\Time\) under continuous utility or site power, an untethered phone must bound total energy (\(\text{Energy} = \text{Power} \times \Time\)) so that every inference fits both the daily battery reserve and the passive thermal envelope of 3–5 W imposed by the power wall (equation 2). In archetype terms, a compute-heavy workload such as ResNet-50 image classification must be replaced by a compact vision architecture,21 cutting FLOPs by 13.7× at a modest accuracy cost so the model can run sustainably without overheating the handset.

Definition 2.4: Mobile ML

Mobile machine learning is the deployment paradigm bounded by thermal design power (TDP) and battery energy.

  1. Significance: It is constrained by the few-watt heat dissipation capacity of passive cooling, requiring architectures that prioritize sustained energy efficiency over peak throughput \((\PeakRate)\).
  2. Distinction: Unlike edge ML, which may have active cooling, mobile ML must operate within a personal energy budget. Unlike TinyML, it still provides a rich OS and multi-watt compute capacity.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that mobile ML performance is a fixed value. In reality, it is a time-varying constraint. Performance often drops as the device hits its thermal wall, triggering thermal throttling that lowers clock frequencies and reduces the active duty cycle.

Two boundaries define the mobile envelope, and together they push the design toward on-device NPU acceleration and model compression (figure 8). The first is the battery tax, the share of the phone’s finite energy budget that an always-on ML feature spends before the rest of the system runs, because the battery limits total energy over time. Thermodynamics imposes the second boundary, which holds even for a plugged-in tablet: every watt becomes heat, and where a data center removes heat with active cooling, a thin mobile device relies on passive transfer through its chassis and surfaces. This creates the thermal wall, the power wall’s device-level form, which sets a hard ceiling on sustained power consumption independent of battery capacity.

Napkin Math 2.8: The battery tax
Problem: Consider deploying a “real-time” background object detector on a smartphone. The model consumes 2 W of continuous power when active. The phone has a standard 15 Wh battery. Can the feature stay on all day? This is the battery tax that turns battery life into a mobile ML energy-budget constraint.

Physics:

  1. Ideal runtime: \(\frac{15\text{ Wh}}{2\text{ W}}\) = 7.5 hours
  2. Reality: A user expects their phone to last 24 hours. Running this single feature continuously for a day would require 320 percent of the phone’s daily energy budget.

Systems insight: The model cannot simply be “deployed.” A lighter model, duty cycling, or both must reduce the energy demand, so the feature can stay on all day.

Sustained thermal performance falls well below burst peaks.

The distinction matters for engineering decisions. The battery tax is a budget problem, solvable in principle by reducing how often the model runs or by increasing battery capacity. The thermal wall is a physics ceiling. A larger battery cannot raise the maximum sustained wattage a passive chassis can dissipate; duty-cycle and software changes can only reduce heat generation. Sustained operation above the thermal envelope triggers hardware throttling regardless of remaining battery energy. The two constraints therefore attack different points in the iron law: the battery limits total operations per charge (\(\Operations\) integrated over time), while the thermal wall caps the instantaneous rate (\(\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw\)) the silicon can sustain.

Napkin Math 2.9: The thermal wall
Problem: An unoptimized vision model requires 12 W peak compute. Can it be deployed on a mobile device, or does it hit the thermal wall and the mobile power wall?

Scenario: A mobile system-on-chip (SoC) allows approximately 3 W for passive cooling. Assume the unoptimized model is deployed as is. A representative throttling trace for this heavier model runs in three steps:

  1. Temperature rise: At 12 W, the SoC junction temperature rises at approximately 1 °C/s.
  2. Thermal trip: Within 60 s, the hardware reaches the thermal trip point (80 °C), triggering OS throttling.
  3. Throttled throughput: The 100 FPS model suddenly drops to 30 FPS to stay within the thermal envelope.

Math: Assume that a redesign (a lighter architecture running INT8 instead of FP32 arithmetic) cuts active power by an illustrative 4×: 12 W ÷ 4 = 3 W, which lands right at the 3 W passive-cooling ceiling with no headroom to spare. The factor is a scenario input for the whole redesign. Quantization alone shrinks weight storage by that factor but does not cut power proportionally, because switching and leakage energy outside the arithmetic remain.

Systems insight: Even a 4× power reduction does not create thermal headroom; it only reaches the sustainable limit. Optimization can bring a model down to the thermal ceiling; it cannot raise the ceiling.

Battery and heat are the two limits a mobile deployment must account for explicitly. Within them, the phone’s hardware envelope determines which models can run and how often.

2.7.1 Mobile ML benefits and resource constraints

Even a 1 W continuous ML workload, half the power draw of the detector in napkin math 2.8, would cut a 24-hour smartphone’s runtime to roughly 9.2 hours–11.5 hours on a 15 Wh–22 Wh battery. Avoiding that drain shapes the hardware envelope in the mobile row of table 4: gigabytes of RAM, neural processing units delivering tens of TOPS, and 3–5 W of sustained power on a system-on-chip22 that integrates computation and memory to minimize data-movement energy. Memory capacity still constrains model footprint and memory bandwidth still limits how quickly weights and activations move, but the shared battery sets the ceiling that users notice first.

22 System-on-chip (SoC): Mobile SoCs integrate CPU, GPU, and NPU cores with caches and memory controllers to reduce data-movement costs. Mobile DRAM is generally off-die, and feasible model size depends on capacity, precision, working memory, bandwidth, and latency.

23 Face ID: Apple performs facial matching within the Secure Enclave and encrypts the stored mathematical representations so that they remain available only to that subsystem and do not leave the device (Apple 2024a, 2024b). Apple reports a random false-match probability below 1 in 1,000,000 for one enrolled appearance, with higher probabilities for some populations and configurations. The example shows how local inference and hardware isolation can reduce transmission exposure while supporting low-latency authentication.

Apple. 2024a. Facial Matching Security. Apple Platform Security.
Apple. 2024b. The Secure Enclave. Apple Platform Security.

Mobile adds two gains to those every local tier shares. The first is hardware isolation for the most sensitive inputs, since on-device enclaves such as Apple’s Secure Enclave keep biometric matching inside a dedicated subsystem,23 although the strength of any privacy guarantee still depends on the complete system design and threat model. The second is personalization, because models can exploit on-device signals and user context without shipping raw data anywhere. Specialized on-device ML frameworks keep such inference within 5–50 ms user-interface response times.

The cost shows up in engineering effort as well as joules. Peak NPU throughput can substantially exceed what the device sustains under prolonged load, device heterogeneity requires multiple model variants, development multiplies across platforms, and app-store review and staged release slow iteration compared with a service its operator controls entirely.

2.7.2 Personal assistant and media processing

Several short ML pipelines on one phone must share the same battery and thermal budget, and computational photography shows that constraint most sharply. Modern flagships process each photo through multiple ML stages: portrait mode uses depth estimation and segmentation, night mode aligns multiple frames with ML-based denoising, and HDR merging, super-resolution, and scene optimization run in sequence. The binding constraint falls on the pipeline rather than on any individual model, because these models must share a 3–5 W power budget and complete within the user’s perceived shutter delay, which requires careful scheduling across CPU, GPU, and NPU to avoid thermal throttling.

Voice-driven interactions are bound by the energy of always-on listening, so they partition work across the same SoC by power tier. Wake-word detection runs continuously on a dedicated low-power core, while speech recognition and keyboard prediction wake the higher-power neural processing tiers only when needed.

Health monitoring and augmented reality push mobile ML to its sustained-performance limits. Wearables like Apple Watch process ECG and accelerometer data on-device to reduce the radio energy of transmission, while AR frameworks must fit simultaneous localization, hand tracking, and scene understanding into a 16.7 ms frame budget at 60 FPS without exceeding the sustained thermal envelope. These applications represent the ceiling of what battery-powered, passively-cooled devices can sustain, and they define the boundary beyond which mobile optimization alone is insufficient.

Respecting that sustained-performance ceiling is why teams cannot deploy desktop-trained models directly to mobile or constrained edge devices without architectural modification. A desktop ResNet-50 pipeline may require gigabytes once activations, batches, preprocessing buffers, and runtime overhead are included, even though the FP32 weights alone are about 102.2 MB. Those weights fit in the 512 MB of RAM on a representative low-end edge target, but at 8.2 GFLOP per inference its 1 GFLOP/s processor takes seconds per inference, a latency failure rather than a capacity one. Desktop-optimized models may also use operations unsupported by mobile hardware, assume numerical formats unavailable on embedded systems, or require batch processing incompatible with single-sample inference. Successful deployment demands architecture-aware design from the beginning, so model families, arithmetic formats, and implementation choices must all match the target device.

↪ The framework that organizes pruning, distillation, and quantization to fit edge memory budgets is developed in Fitting the Deployment Budget.

Mobile ML shows that useful intelligence fits within a 3–5 W thermal envelope on battery power. A smartphone still costs hundreds of dollars, carries gigabytes of memory, and needs a daily recharge, and no amount of mobile optimization closes the roughly 10,000-fold memory gap to a device that must cost dollars and run unattended for years. That regime needs a different class of hardware and algorithms.

Self-Check: Question

  1. How do the battery tax and the thermal wall differ as physical constraints on mobile ML systems?

    1. The battery tax is an energy budget problem bounding total operations over time (\(\int \text{Power} \cdot dt\)), whereas the thermal wall is a heat dissipation ceiling bounding instantaneous sustained power (\(\text{Power} \le 3\text{--}5\text{ W}\)) regardless of battery size.
    2. The battery tax applies only to iOS devices, while the thermal wall applies only to Android devices.
    3. The battery tax can be resolved by lowering clock frequency, but the thermal wall requires increasing the clock frequency to finish calculations faster.
    4. The battery tax represents static memory leakage in SRAM, while the thermal wall represents network transmission power in 5G modems.

  2. Why does deploying a mobile computational photography feature (such as Portrait Mode or Night Mode) present a pipeline scheduling challenge rather than a single-model optimization problem?

    1. Mobile cameras can only run one neural network per battery charge cycle.
    2. The feature executes a multi-stage pipeline (depth estimation, semantic segmentation, alignment, and HDR tone mapping) that must all complete within a perceived shutter delay while sharing a 3–5 W thermal budget across CPU, GPU, and NPU.
    3. Computational photography models cannot use quantized INT8 representations due to mathematical rounding artifacts in lens optics.
    4. Camera image signal processors (ISPs) operate asynchronously from the operating system and reject all external ML tensor inputs.

  3. Why does deploying an unoptimized desktop-trained vision model (such as an FP32 ResNet-50 pipeline) directly onto a mobile device frequently fail, even when the model’s FP32 weights (~102.2 MB) appear smaller than the phone’s total RAM?

  4. Running biometric matching (such as Face ID) inside a dedicated on-device Secure Enclave reduces data disclosure risks by keeping mathematical representations isolated in encrypted hardware, but it does not automatically eliminate all security or privacy risks without rigorous end-to-end system design.

  5. Order the sequence of events that occurs when an unoptimized 12 W deep learning model runs continuously on a passively cooled 3 W smartphone SoC:

  1. The device temperature rises rapidly (e.g., at ~1 °C per second) as power dissipation exceeds passive cooling capacity
  2. The unoptimized model launches at full burst performance (e.g., 100 FPS) drawing 12 W of active compute power
  3. Operating system and hardware thermal controllers engage thermal throttling, reducing clock frequencies and duty cycles
  4. The internal SoC junction temperature reaches the critical thermal trip point (e.g., 80 °C)
  5. Sustained inference throughput drops precipitously (e.g., from 100 FPS to 30 FPS) to stabilize power within the 3 W TDP envelope

See Answers →

2.8 TinyML: Ubiquitous Sensing

Instrumenting every pallet in a warehouse, every cable on a suspension bridge, or every beehive in an apiary means putting “eyes and ears” on tens of thousands to millions of physical objects. At that count, each device must cost dollars rather than hundreds of dollars and measure millimeters rather than centimeters. Smartphones are far too expensive and too large for the role; ubiquitous sensing at this scale requires unit costs under ten dollars.

TinyML completes the deployment spectrum with low-cost, low-power ML on deeply constrained embedded devices (Warden and Situnayake 2020; Janapa Reddi et al. 2022). Devices costing under $10 and averaging less than one milliwatt24 of power when duty-cycled make ubiquitous25 sensing economically practical at large scale. MLPerf Tiny and MCUNet show how such constraints are evaluated and optimized in practice (Banbury et al. 2021; Lin et al. 2020). Its first feasibility screen is memory capacity, because weights must fit in flash and working state in SRAM, but once a model fits, power is the limit that shapes the design. This is the domain of the power-bound archetype, where the optimization objective shifts from maximizing throughput to minimizing energy per inference. With duty-cycled execution, a keyword spotting model consuming about 10 µJ per inference on dedicated low-power audio silicon can operate for years on a coin-cell battery, trading model capacity for operational longevity. A general-purpose microcontroller running the same model spends orders of magnitude more. On an Arduino Nano 33 BLE Sense development board (figure 9), on-board microphone, motion, and environmental sensors share a single substrate with a low-power microcontroller executing quantized TensorFlow Lite Micro models (David et al. 2021). The board illustrates integration; its full power draw depends on the sensors, radio, and duty cycle.

Warden, Pete, and Daniel Situnayake. 2020. TinyML: Machine Learning with TensorFlow Lite on Arduino and Ultra-Low-Power Microcontrollers. O’Reilly Media.

24 The 1 mW threshold: Ambient energy harvesting can power a device indefinitely only when long-term harvested power exceeds the complete system’s average load. Available power varies across sources and environments. Solar cells the size of a thumbnail may produce ~10 mW outdoors but ~10 µW indoors, thermoelectric generators on warm pipes ~100 µW, and RF energy from nearby transmitters ~10 µW. This crossover can transform the deployment model from “battery-limited lifetime” to “deploy and forget,” but it depends on the source, environment, and workload.

25 Ubiquitous computing: Mark Weiser’s ubiquitous-computing vision imagined computation woven into everyday environments until it receded from direct attention (Weiser 1991). TinyML is one modern path toward that vision, because it brings the cost and power of an intelligent sensor low enough for mass deployment.

Weiser, Mark. 1991. “The Computer for the 21st Century.” Scientific American 265 (3): 94–104. https://doi.org/10.1038/scientificamerican0991-94.
David, Robert, Jared Duke, Advait Jain, Vijay Janapa Reddi, Nat Jeffries, Jian Li, Nick Kreeger, et al. 2021. “TensorFlow Lite Micro: Embedded Machine Learning for TinyML Systems.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems 3: 800–811.

TinyML runs on microcontrollers (MCUs),26 which omit the power-hungry infrastructure of conventional computing. Mobile and edge processors rely on external dynamic RAM (DRAM), high-speed memory buses, and operating system runtimes that draw milliwatts just to keep DRAM in self-refresh and hundreds of milliwatts once the memory bus is active. Many microcontrollers integrate the processor core, non-volatile flash, and static RAM (SRAM) onto a single silicon die, reducing reliance on external memory buses (Banbury et al. 2021; Lin et al. 2020; Janapa Reddi et al. 2022). Eliminating off-chip signaling lets duty-cycled average power fall below a milliwatt and sleep power reach microwatts, although active inference on boards such as the ESP32-S3 still draws tens to hundreds of milliwatts. Some targets, including ESP32-S3 variants, instead attach external flash or additional RAM. On-chip integration still imposes a strict internal-memory ceiling. On-chip SRAM is physically constrained by silicon die area: because each SRAM cell requires six transistors (\(6\text{T}\)), expanding capacity directly inflates manufacturing cost. A TinyML model must therefore execute within tens to hundreds of kilobytes of SRAM, with no virtual memory, paging, or swap space to cushion an overflow. The small keyword-spotting task and low-power hardware place it at the bottom of figure 7. Duty cycle also determines its average power.27 Multi-year coin-cell operation additionally requires the complete device load to fit the battery budget.28 Fitting the entire inference pipeline, from sensor acquisition to classification output, into this kilobyte-scale memory footprint defines the TinyML operating regime.

26 Microcontroller (MCU): A single-chip computer whose design prioritizes minimal cost and power over performance. Memory fit therefore becomes a deployment prerequisite, because the memory actually available to the target is often orders of magnitude less than a smartphone provides.

Banbury, Colby, Vijay Janapa Reddi, Peter Torelli, Jeremy Holleman, Nat Jeffries, Csaba Kiraly, Pietro Montino, et al. 2021. “MLPerf Tiny Benchmark.” arXiv Preprint.
Lin, Ji, Wei-Ming Chen, Yujun Lin, John Cohn, Chuang Gan, and Song Han. 2020. “MCUNet: Tiny Deep Learning on IoT Devices.” In Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems 2020, NeurIPS 2020, December 6-12, 2020, Virtual, edited by Hugo Larochelle, Marc’Aurelio Ranzato, Raia Hadsell, Maria-Florina Balcan, and Hsuan-Tien Lin. Curran Associates.
Janapa Reddi, Vijay, Brian Plancher, Susan Kennedy, Laurence Moroney, Pete Warden, Lara Suzuki, Anant Agarwal, et al. 2022. “Widening Access to Applied Machine Learning with TinyML.” Harvard Data Science Review 4 (1). https://doi.org/10.1162/99608f92.762d171a.

27 TinyML energy gap: The figure compares different tasks on different hardware and with different energy accounting boundaries; its ratios do not identify the effect of hardware integration alone. A batch-1 ResNet-50 inference on a cloud accelerator consumes about 75.5 mJ of accelerator energy, while an always-on keyword spotter uses about 10 µJ, a gap of ~7,500×. A cloud LLM query at about 1 kJ widens the gap to 100,000,000×, the span plotted in figure 7.

28 Coin-cell deployment: A CR2032 battery (225 mAh at 3 V, ~675 mWh) ideally powers a complete 10–50 µW system for about 1.5–7.7 years. This “deploy-and-forget” operating model drives innovation in intermittent computing, where the device sleeps between inferences to stretch the energy budget across years of unattended operation.

Definition 2.5: TinyML

TinyML is the machine learning domain of always-on sensing constrained by kilobyte-scale memory and milliwatt-scale power.

  1. Significance: It requires models whose weights and code fit in flash while activations and runtime state fit in on-chip SRAM, enabling continuous inference on milliwatt power budgets.
  2. Distinction: Unlike mobile ML, which uses multi-watt processors and a full OS, TinyML runs on microcontrollers using bare-metal firmware or a real-time operating system.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that TinyML is just “small models.” In reality, it is a power-bound regime, and the primary metric is energy per inference (microjoules), not just the parameter count.

This definition reframes the iron law of ML systems. In terms of equation 5, TinyML imposes a physical memory-capacity precondition that execution time does not capture: the weight footprint \(\WeightMemory\) and activation footprint \(\ActivationMemory\) must stay within memory capacity \(\DeviceMemoryCapacity\). When total memory is measured in kilobytes, model structure and runtime allocation determine feasibility before latency optimization begins, and every avoidable data movement spends part of the energy budget. The objective therefore shifts from raw throughput toward energy per inference and memory fit.

The same four-branch map applies at the bottom of the spectrum (figure 10). Milliwatt budgets and kilobyte memories favor low-precision execution and duty-cycled sleep, and they move the failure boundary away from latency toward compression-driven accuracy loss and risky firmware updates.

2.8.1 TinyML advantages and operational trade-offs

Stripping away the continuous radio uplink and the application processor is what lets TinyML devices shrink from palm-sized developer kits to millimeter-scale chips29 that cost single-digit dollars and run for months or years on a coin-cell battery or harvested ambient energy. That same hardware austerity, however, removes the memory headroom required to train models on the device,30 so models are trained elsewhere and deployed through specialized algorithms, aggressive model compression, and tight hardware-software co-design.

29 TinyML device range: This physical range reflects a direct trade-off between deployment context and computational capability. Millimeter-scale systems prioritize minimal power for single-function, long-duration tasks, whereas palm-sized boards offer more sensing, interfaces, and debugging support at higher cost and power. Development-board size and power therefore should not be treated as limits for a finished TinyML product.

30 On-device training constraints: Updating a model requires working memory beyond that needed to apply it, including information retained from the prediction computation to determine parameter changes. Intermediate results can be discarded only after their final use, which often occurs later during training than during inference. With only 256 KB–2 MB RAM, many microcontrollers cannot hold the full training state; specialized methods reduce that state by limiting which parameters change. TinyTL, for example, freezes the weights and updates mainly the biases, so most intermediate activations need not be retained for the update (Cai et al. 2020). Training elsewhere and delivering an updated model is therefore a common design, although constrained local adaptation remains possible.

Cai, Han, Chuang Gan, Ligeng Zhu, and Song Han. 2020. “TinyTL: Reduce Activations, Not Trainable Parameters for Efficient on-Device Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 11285–97.

The trade-offs are just as distinctive. Microcontrollers commonly provide \(10^5\) to \(10^6\) bytes of RAM and enforce a strict two-tier memory partition. Static model weights and compiled code reside in non-volatile flash (often a few megabytes). Mutable activations and the runtime buffer (the tensor arena, a fixed block of SRAM reserved for them) must fit within the much smaller on-chip SRAM. That SRAM holds a few hundred kilobytes at most, such as the ESP32-S3’s 512 KB, and as little as tens of kilobytes on the smallest parts. As a result, peak intermediate activation volume, rather than static parameter storage, is frequently the barrier that prevents a model from executing. The smart doorbell’s level-1 check (figure 3) shows why that check is only a first screen: its weights fill just 11.9 percent of flash, a figure that says nothing about whether its intermediate results fit in SRAM. Embedded development also demands cross-stack co-design: engineers manage memory pools by hand, debug bare-metal firmware without operating-system abstractions, and optimize operator kernels directly for constrained instruction sets.

These bounds also create lifecycle and reliability challenges. An energy-harvesting device can satisfy its long-term average power budget yet fail when stored energy is exhausted before the next harvest. Its duty cycle must therefore respect both energy per inference and the timing of available power. Model quality can also suffer from aggressive quantization and pruning: converting 32-bit floating-point weights to 8-bit integers (INT8) saves memory but can degrade accuracy and robustness on out-of-distribution sensor inputs. Post-deployment flexibility is also sharply constrained: models are often packaged with firmware, so field updates must account for limited storage, interrupted transfers, and safe rollback. Ecosystem fragmentation31 across microcontroller vendors and ML frameworks creates additional overhead and portability challenges.

31 TinyML ecosystem fragmentation: TinyML spans microcontroller families such as Arm Cortex-M, RISC-V, and Xtensa, each with different instruction sets, memory layouts, kernels, and vendor toolchains; cloud and mobile stacks also vary, but their runtimes usually hide more of this hardware diversity. A model optimized for one microcontroller target may therefore require retuning and revalidation for another, multiplying the engineering cost of multi-device deployment.

2.8.2 Environmental and health monitoring

In consumer devices, always-on wake-word detection uses that milliwatt envelope as a continuous power gate that wakes costlier application processors only when a trigger phrase is heard.32 In environmental and health monitoring, the same local-filtering pattern governs sensors deployed where unit economics and physical isolation rule out higher tiers altogether. Across large agricultural fields, sparse and expensive connectivity binds first; FarmBeats used sensors, cameras, drones, and local gateway processing to reduce raw data movement in precision agriculture (Vasisht et al. 2017), and TinyML pushes that filtering down to individual sensing nodes running under milliwatt budgets.

32 Always-on wake-word detection: A small model that listens continuously for the acoustic signature of a wake phrase. The acceptable detector budget depends on the platform, but spending even a few extra milliwatts continuously can materially shorten battery life, and the architecture succeeds only when the always-on path consumes far less average power than the components it prevents from waking.

Vasisht, Deepak, Zerina Kapetanovic, Jongho Won, Xinxin Jin, Ranveer Chandra, Sudipta N. Sinha, Ashish Kapoor, Madhusudhan Sudarshan, and Sean Stratman. 2017. “FarmBeats: An IoT Platform for Data-Driven Agriculture.” 14th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 17), 515–29.

Wildlife conservation shows the uplink arithmetic in an illustrative case. Solar-powered acoustic sensors identify species on-device and transmit periodic detection summaries rather than raw audio, cutting uplink data from 4.3 GB/day to 400 KB/day, a 10,750× reduction that conserves power in off-grid deployments. Medical wearables apply the same logic to physiological monitoring, where runtime remains bounded by sensor draw, radio power, duty cycle, and sleep energy rather than by the model alone.

All four paradigms sit on one continuum bounded by the speed of light, heat dissipation, and memory signaling, and a real feature usually presses against several of those bounds at once. Choosing among the paradigms therefore means mapping an application’s constraints against all of them together.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Comparing a dedicated TinyML keyword spotting model (~\(10\ \mu\text{J}\) per inference) to a cloud foundation model query (~\(1\text{ kJ}\) per inference), by approximately what factor does energy consumption per inference differ?

    1. Approximately \(100\times\) (2 orders of magnitude)
    2. Approximately \(10{,}000\times\) (4 orders of magnitude)
    3. Approximately \(100{,}000{,}000\times\) (\(10^8\), 8 orders of magnitude)
    4. Approximately \(10^{14}\times\) (14 orders of magnitude)

  2. Why are microcontroller TinyML devices predominantly restricted to inference-only execution rather than full in-situ backpropagation training?

    1. Microcontrollers use flash memory which permanently locks all transistor gates against state changes.
    2. Backpropagation requires double-precision FP64 floating-point arithmetic which is mathematically impossible on 32-bit cores.
    3. The speed of light inside microcontroller silicon is too slow to support backward gradient propagation.
    4. Conventional training retains intermediate results needed for gradient calculations and adds gradient and optimizer state, which can exceed a microcontroller’s working-memory budget.

  3. Why is the 1 mW average power threshold considered a transformative milestone in TinyML system deployment?

  4. Why is it a fallacy to treat TinyML as merely ‘scaled-down mobile ML’ that can be addressed simply by applying standard INT8 post-training quantization?

  5. The power management paradigm in which a battery-less or energy-harvesting microcontroller sleeps in ultra-low-power states and wakes periodically to perform inference before energy reserves deplete is called ____ computing.

See Answers →

2.9 Choosing a Deployment Paradigm

An architect choosing where to run an ML feature rarely optimizes one dimension. A privacy rule may forbid cloud processing, a latency budget may forbid remote inference, a memory footprint may exceed the mobile device, and a cost target may rule out always-on edge hardware. Cloud, edge, mobile, and TinyML are operating envelopes for those conflicts, so selecting among them requires a unified comparison framework and a structured decision process.

2.9.1 Weighing operational trade-offs across tiers

A model that fits comfortably inside a tier’s latency, power, and memory budgets can still fail in production if the team cannot ship updates to it, if a dropped internet link halts the service, or if monthly cloud bills outrun the product’s revenue. Choosing a deployment tier requires weighing latency-vs-throughput paradigm trade-offs across physical and operational budgets at once. The resource envelopes are already on the table: table 1 gives each paradigm’s latency, power, and memory regime, and table 4 grounds them in concrete platforms. What those tables leave out are the operational dimensions that decide many real deployments: how the system scales, what it exposes, whether it needs a network, what it costs, and how hard it is to build and ship. Table 9 contrasts the four paradigms along those eight dimensions.

Remote processing widens the network attack surface and requires transport encryption and strict isolation between tenants; local processing keeps raw data on the device, reducing network exposure without eliminating the risk of physical tampering. The workload archetypes from section 2.3 map directly to these boundaries, with each archetype gravitating toward paradigms that relieve its primary bottleneck. Each paradigm also shifts engineering complexity to a different subsystem. Cloud deployments demand expertise in multi-tenant networking and dynamic cost governance, whereas edge and TinyML deployments trade those cloud concerns for local thermal and memory limits along with distributed updates and site maintenance. Mobile deployments sit between the two, operating on user-owned hardware that avoids infrastructure purchase costs at the price of platform-specific drivers, operating-system background task limits, and shared energy budgets. None of the four categories therefore supplies a workload-independent latency or energy ranking.

A common architectural failure is selecting a deployment target based on model accuracy alone while treating hardware constraints as downstream optimization details. An unquantized model that delivers 99 percent accuracy in an offline evaluation becomes unviable if its memory footprint triggers paging on mobile hardware, or if continuous inference drives thermal throttling within seconds. Physical invariants (memory capacity, thermal dissipation limits, and network transit delay) must serve as early screening criteria before model development begins, pruning invalid deployment targets before training resources are expended.

2.9.2 Four screening gates for choosing a tier

Pruning invalid targets begins one step earlier than choosing between the cloud and a microcontroller: before committing to any deployment tier, an engineer must verify whether the problem requires machine learning at all. Deep neural networks carry a heavy maintenance burden that deterministic code avoids, and a fifty-line heuristic rule set can often solve the problem at a tiny fraction of the operating cost.

Systems Perspective 2.3: The complexity tax
Consider a classification task solvable either by deterministic heuristic rules or by a deep learning pipeline. The heuristic requires fifty lines of code, incurs near-zero compute cost, demands approximately one hour per month to update rules, and fails in ways engineers can read in the code. The ML system may require only fifty lines of model definition, but, as Sculley et al. (2015) observed of production ML generally, it introduces roughly 2,000 lines of supporting infrastructure for feature ingestion, telemetry logging, drift monitoring, and GPU driver management. It also requires 40 hours per month diagnosing data drift and maintaining pipelines.

An ML model that increases accuracy from 90 percent to 95 percent while multiplying operational maintenance by, say, 40\(\times\) requires an economic comparison. The accuracy and maintenance ratios alone do not establish the return; the value of the avoided errors must justify the additional engineering cost. When the ongoing maintenance cost outstrips the value of marginal statistical gains, deterministic software remains the correct systems choice.

Sculley, D., Gary Holt, Daniel Golovin, Eugene Davydov, Todd Phillips, Dietmar Ebner, Vinay Chaudhary, Michael Young, Jean-François Crespo, and Dan Dennison. 2015. “Hidden Technical Debt in Machine Learning Systems.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 28: 2503–11.

↪ Framing the problem against the existing baseline, and retaining that baseline when the expected benefit does not justify the added cost, is developed in Stage 1: Problem Definition.

When this complexity tax outweighs the operational value of improved statistical predictions, the ML deployment costs the system more than it returns. Once a learned system is justified, however, paradigm selection relies on a tiered decision framework ordered by how rapidly a constraint invalidates an architecture. The screening hierarchy evaluates four successive gates: regulatory privacy, physical latency, local compute capacity, and lifecycle economics. Privacy leads the order because a legal prohibition on moving data eliminates an option before anything is measured, whereas the latency and compute gates require numbers. Each gate passes only its survivors to the next, so the screening sequence acts as successive filters on a candidate set (figure 11).

The flowchart carries a remaining candidate set through all four gates. If regulatory rules or data sovereignty forbid off-device transmission, remote cloud processing stays excluded when compute demand is considered. If a network round-trip lower bound exceeds the deadline, additional remote compute cannot restore that path. Cost compares only the survivors, and an empty set calls for changing the workload or partitioning it across tiers. Passing a lower-bound latency screen still requires complete-path validation. In automotive emergency braking, the latency gate binds before compute capacity or cost are even considered.

Example 2.1: Autonomous vehicle emergency braking
Scenario: An autonomous vehicle vision system performs real-time pedestrian detection for emergency braking under a strict 100 ms end-to-end latency budget.

Diagnosis: At 100 km/h, the vehicle covers 2.8 m during the 100 ms budget, so every millisecond of delay is distance traveled before braking begins. The scenario assumes 50–150 ms of network round-trip delay for cloud offload, which consumes half the budget at best and more than all of it at worst before any detection runs. Local object detection on an automotive edge accelerator takes 10–30 ms; at 10 GFLOPs per frame and 30 FPS, that accelerator must sustain 300 GFLOP/s.

Systems lesson: The stated network-delay range cannot guarantee the deadline, so the latency gate excludes that cloud path. The local candidates remain subject to the resource gate: the pedestrian detector must fit memory and sustain the stated frame rate within its power budget. An automotive edge accelerator remains a candidate under these assumptions; its complete sensing-to-braking path still needs validation. Cloud connections can support offline training and telemetry without reentering the emergency decision path.

A completed placement decision needs evidence about the surviving paths as well as grounds for rejecting the others. On a factory inspection line, the four gates first eliminate remote and underpowered tiers and then select between two feasible edge designs on matched lifecycle cost.

Example 2.2: Completing a placement decision
Problem: A fixed factory camera requires 20 FPS inspection with a 100 ms sensing-to-decision deadline. Raw images must remain on premises. Which feasible deployment has the lowest matched lifecycle cost under the following hypothetical evidence?

Supplied evidence: A rule-based baseline fails the specified missed-defect criterion, while the learned detector meets both the missed-defect and false-alarm criteria on representative inspection data. The proposed mobile and microcontroller variants fail that same quality requirement. Local edge candidates A and B preserve the validated detector and preprocessing. Both have a complete peak working set of 2 GB within an available 4 GB, and each must stay within a 25 W sustained system-power budget.

Under the specified peak load and thermal conditions, the supplied complete-path latency bounds are 80 ms for A and 40 ms for B, including acquisition, preprocessing, queuing, inference, and the inspection decision. Their sustained rates are 25 FPS and 40 FPS, at 10 W and 20 W. Both satisfy the same availability and recovery requirements. Annualized budgets over the same service lifetime, including hardware, power, installation, maintenance, and recovery provisions, are $2,400 for A and $3,600 for B. These supplied bounds and budgets define an instructional scenario, not benchmarks of named products.

Result: Select local edge candidate A. Privacy excludes raw-image cloud offload, task quality excludes the proposed smaller variants, and both edge paths satisfy the timing, throughput, memory, and power requirements. A has the lower matched cost. B’s faster response supplies no required benefit under this specification; a tighter deadline could change the choice.

Systems lesson: The final choice follows from task quality and complete-path evidence, not the tier label or peak compute rate. The decision remains valid within the supplied operating conditions; changes to the input stream, detector, load, or thermal environment require reevaluating the relevant gates.

The completed comparison depends on operational maintainability as well as execution performance. Its matched budgets include obligations that a hardware-only comparison would omit. Deploying a model across thousands of edge nodes or millions of microcontrollers introduces sustained lifecycle overhead: automated firmware updates, out-of-band telemetry, distributed drift monitoring, and hardware failure recovery. An organization equipped for cloud operations may lack the embedded toolchains and physical testing infrastructure required to support edge or TinyML targets at scale.

Checkpoint 2.2: System design

First justify the learned system against simpler baselines; then screen its deployment candidates against the physical and operational constraints.

Decision gates

The decision framework screens candidate tiers for physical feasibility and then compares their operational costs. If no single tier meets the requirements of the whole pipeline, the next question is which stages can run separately, and what must cross the boundaries between them.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In the four-layer deployment screening decision framework, why is the Privacy layer evaluated first, followed by Latency, Compute Demand, and Cost?

    1. The layers are ordered by how rapidly a hard constraint can completely invalidate an architecture: legal/privacy rules may forbid remote transmission entirely, physical latency limits can make cloud offload impossible, compute demands eliminate undersized devices, and cost compares the remaining feasible survivors.
    2. Privacy is the easiest parameter to optimize in software using compiler flags, whereas cost cannot be calculated until after deployment.
    3. Cloud providers require privacy agreements before allowing access to GPU instances.
    4. The framework is ordered purely alphabetically based on traditional software engineering conventions.

  2. An autonomous vehicle travels at 100 km/h (~27.8 m/s). An emergency pedestrian detection system evaluates two architectures: (1) an automotive Edge ML accelerator with a 15 ms local detection latency, and (2) a Cloud ML server with 115 ms total latency (100 ms network RTT + 15 ms compute). How much additional distance does the vehicle travel before braking begins if the cloud architecture is used?

    1. 0.28 meters
    2. Approximately 2.8 meters
    3. Approximately 28 meters
    4. Exactly 100 meters

  3. What is the complexity tax in ML systems engineering, and when is a simple heuristic (e.g., rule-based logic or regular expressions) superior to a deep learning pipeline?

  4. In ML systems engineering, selecting a deployment paradigm based solely on benchmark model accuracy is a sound practice because hardware infrastructure can always be scaled to meet application constraints later.

  5. What is the Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) calculation, and why must ML architects evaluate TCO rather than hardware purchase price or cloud VM hourly rates alone when selecting a deployment paradigm?

See Answers →

2.10 Hybrid Architectures

A voice interface can use a microcontroller to listen continuously for a wake word, a phone to transcribe speech, and cloud servers to interpret requests that need a larger model. Each stage has a different task and resource budget. Sending every audio sample to the cloud would add communication and radio costs; asking the microcontroller to perform every stage would exceed its compute and memory capacity. Hybrid architectures divide the pipeline across tiers so that each stage meets its requirements (Kang et al. 2017). Economics drive this division as much as latency: processing voice interactions for billions of devices entirely in the cloud runs into both an economic and an infrastructure ceiling, and the voice assistant wall puts a number on each.

Kang, Yiping, Johann Hauswald, Cao Gao, Austin Rovinski, Trevor Mudge, Jason Mars, and Lingjia Tang. 2017. “Neurosurgeon.” International Conference on Architectural Support for Programming Languages and Operating Systems (ASPLOS), 615–29. https://doi.org/10.1145/3037697.3037698.

Napkin Math 2.10: The voice assistant wall
Problem: In this illustrative capacity scenario, 1B voice assistant devices (smartphones, smart speakers, earbuds) each issue 20 queries/day after the wake word. Detecting the wake word itself in the cloud would require every microphone to stream audio continuously. What load would cloud-side detection impose, and what load remains in the cloud once detection moves on device?

Cloud-side detection: The always-listening path that local detection removes.

  • Audio streaming: Every device streams audio continuously (16 kHz, 16-bit), about 32 KB/s each. Across 1 billion devices the aggregate is 32 TB/s, and each stream would also need its own continuously running detector.
  • Cloud cost: ~$0.50/device/year → 1B devices = $500,000,000/year. Economically prohibitive under these assumptions for a free feature.
  • TinyML alternative: 0.1–1 mW local wake-word detection, <$0.01/device/year. If each post-wake query uploads about 5 s of audio, local detection cuts the audio leaving each device by about 860×.

Residual cloud load: The post-wake queries the hybrid design still serves in the cloud. Even with local wake-word filtering, the residual query volume (1 billion devices generating 20B queries/day at ~200 ms per query) still requires substantial centralized accelerator capacity (~1.1 million GPU-hours per day).

Five parameters determine the required cluster capacity:

  1. Query volume: 1 billion devices \(\times\) 20 queries/day = 20B queries/day.
  2. GPU demand: Each query requires ~200 ms of GPU time. Total: 1.1 million GPU-hours/day.
  3. Scenario capacity: A data-center equivalent provisioned with 10,000 GPUs provides 240,000 GPU-hours/day.
  4. Average requirement: ~4.6 dedicated data centers just for voice inference.
  5. Peak reality: Queries cluster in waking hours (~4.5× peak-to-average), requiring ~20.8 data centers at peak.

Systems insight: Local detection reduces each device’s upload to its post-wake queries, 20 queries/day of a few seconds each. The residual post-wake load is still large, tens of data centers at peak, but it is bounded by what users actually ask. It is the load the hybrid design deliberately keeps in the cloud, to use a larger language model than the local device can support.

Partitioning the voice pipeline into an on-device wake-word filter and a cloud-based language model relieves both the client’s energy limit and the data center’s capacity limit. The division also creates engineering work: deciding what data crosses each boundary and keeping the participating stages consistent. Three integration patterns organize these choices.

2.10.1 Splitting work across tiers: Three integration patterns

When a single tier cannot satisfy every stage of an ML pipeline, engineers split the pipeline along one of three physical boundaries: train-serve split, hierarchical processing, or progressive deployment. Selecting a pattern is an iron-law decision: each stage of computation must execute where its binding term (throughput capacity \(\PeakRate\), local latency floor \(\Latency\), or the memory-capacity precondition) incurs the lowest system penalty.

Under a train-serve split, training executes in centralized data centers while inference runs locally on edge, mobile, or embedded devices. Training must retain intermediate results and information used to calculate and apply parameter updates, in addition to the model weights. Under common 16-bit mixed-precision training with a standard adaptive optimizer, the weights, their gradients, and the optimizer’s running statistics together occupy about 16 bytes per parameter (Rajbhandari et al. 2020). That is roughly 8\(\times\) the 2 bytes per parameter that 16-bit inference weights need, before any batch activations are counted (Memory management develops this accounting). Inference uses the trained weights to compute predictions, sometimes through repeated passes as in token-by-token generation, and must satisfy local latency and privacy constraints. Amortization underpins the economics of this split: the high fixed cost of training is distributed across millions of forward-pass queries served locally without recurring network transmission costs.33

Rajbhandari, Samyam, Jeff Rasley, Olatunji Ruwase, and Yuxiong He. 2020. “ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models.” SC20: International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis, 1–16. https://doi.org/10.1109/sc41405.2020.00024.

33 Train-serve cost asymmetry: Training usually requires many forward and backward passes, whereas one inference request uses a trained artifact for one or more forward passes. Training is not literally one-time because models may be retrained, and inference cost varies sharply with model size, sequence length, batching, and service overhead. The economic rationale for the split is amortization: repeated use can spread each training cycle’s fixed cost across many served predictions.

Definition 2.6: Hybrid ML

Hybrid machine learning is the deployment strategy that distributes an ML pipeline across two or more deployment tiers, assigning each stage according to its latency, compute, data, and operational constraints.

  1. Significance: Hybrid architectures exploit the iron law’s additive structure: the edge tier can reduce \(\Latency\) for time-sensitive preprocessing and inference, while the cloud tier can provide the \(\PeakRate\) needed for training, retraining, and heavy batch inference. The split follows the data locality invariant: compare each stage’s complete local and remote paths, reject paths that miss the service deadline, and then choose among the feasible alternatives.
  2. Distinction: Unlike cloud-only deployment (which accepts the distance penalty for all stages) or edge-only deployment (which accepts limited \(\PeakRate\) for all stages), hybrid ML assigns each pipeline stage to the tier where its binding iron law term is minimized.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that hybrid ML is just “running two models.” In reality, the participating tiers must share synchronized state (feature definitions, model versions, and preprocessing logic) so that their paths produce consistent results. Without this synchronization, training-serving skew emerges at tier boundaries.

Under hierarchical processing, computation cascades across tiers to filter and summarize data step by step, matching communication volume to available link bandwidth. Raw sensor streams from cameras or microphones at the edge of the network often exceed wide-area network (WAN) uplink capacity; continuously streaming uncompressed telemetry to a data center saturates network links and wastes radio power. To prevent network saturation, early tiers filter data locally. TinyML microcontrollers run low-power anomaly detectors, edge gateways aggregate and correlate temporal features across multiple sensors, and cloud clusters ingest only anomalous events or aggregated summaries for global analytics. During inference serving, hierarchical processing frequently takes the form of a confidence cascade (Teerapittayanon et al. 2017). A compact, low-latency model on the local device evaluates the input first. If its prediction confidence exceeds a calibrated confidence threshold, the device returns the result immediately, bypassing the network entirely. Only ambiguous or low-confidence inputs trigger a WAN payload transfer to a larger cloud model. The cascade seeks lower median latency at the required task quality, but confident local errors can bypass escalation. Representative evaluation must establish the routed system’s accuracy, error costs, and escalation frequency before accepting that trade-off.

Teerapittayanon, Surat, Bradley McDanel, and Hsiang-Tsung Kung. 2017. “Distributed Deep Neural Networks over the Cloud, the Edge and End Devices.” IEEE International Conference on Distributed Computing Systems (ICDCS), 328–39. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICDCS.2017.226.

Under progressive deployment, an engineering team derives a family of specialized model variants from a unified architecture or training corpus, tailoring each variant to the memory capacity, arithmetic intensity, and thermal limits of a specific tier. Rather than forcing a single model onto every device, developers produce tier-specific artifacts and evaluate the quality lost or retained as resource demands fall. The compression mechanisms are developed later; the placement decision here depends on whether each artifact meets its task and hardware constraints. For a shared image-classification task, an edge server and a phone can use different members of a model family, each evaluated against the same task requirements and its own resource budget. A voice interface that divides wake-word detection, acoustic processing, and language understanding among tiers illustrates hierarchical processing across different tasks, and it can also use tier-specific variants within a task.

With three integration patterns available, selection becomes a constraint-matching problem: choose the pattern whose trade-off profile matches the system’s dominant bottleneck, weighing the conditions that favor each pattern against those that disqualify it (table 10).

Production deployments rarely implement these patterns in isolation; complex systems compose them across different phases of the pipeline. An autonomous vehicle, for example, combines progressive deployment to fit specialized vision backbones into its on-board compute envelope. It uses hierarchical processing to arbitrate between immediate millisecond-scale emergency braking on local hardware and multi-second trajectory planning. It also applies a train-serve split that uploads anomalous driving slices to cloud clusters for fleet-level retraining. Privacy-preserving distributed training approaches extend this composition further when raw training data cannot leave client devices for regulatory or privacy reasons. In federated learning (McMahan et al. 2017), for example, each device trains on its own data and sends only model updates to the cloud for aggregation.

McMahan, Brendan, Eider Moore, Daniel Ramage, Seth Hampson, and Blaise Agüera y Arcas. 2017. “Communication-Efficient Learning of Deep Networks from Decentralized Data.” International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 54: 1273–82.

2.10.2 How hybrid tiers communicate in production

If a phone’s audio preprocessor updates its normalization constants while the cloud transcription model expects the old scaling, or if a smartwatch and phone disagree on which activity labels are active, the hybrid pipeline fails silently across the tier boundary. Keeping multiple execution tiers working as one system requires five explicit communication channels (figure 12). Compiled model artifacts deploy downward from cloud training clusters to edge, mobile, and TinyML inference engines, while raw sensor streams and filtered telemetry propagate upward toward cloud analytics. Between adjacent local tiers, devices forward intermediate inference results and offload heavier queries through assist handoffs, while continuous state synchronization keeps feature definitions, preprocessing constants, and model versions aligned across every boundary.

In the factory, agricultural, and wearable domains introduced earlier in the chapter, production systems place each tier boundary where a single-tier deployment hits its physical limit:

  1. Industrial defect detection: Assembly lines running at high cycle rates require sub-10 ms deterministic visual inspection. Streaming high-resolution camera frames over WAN to a data center violates inspection deadlines and exhausts factory uplink bandwidth. The system deploys a train-serve split: centralized cloud infrastructure trains high-capacity vision models on historical defect databases, then compiles and quantizes runtime weights for factory-floor edge servers and embedded smart cameras, which run inference locally without external network dependencies. The complete local pipeline, including frame acquisition, preprocessing, and inference, must still be validated under the expected load.
  2. Agricultural monitoring: Deployed across hundreds of hectares, remote soil probes run on coin-cell batteries or small solar harvesters and communicate over low-power wide-area radios such as LoRa, which carry little data and spend much energy per bit. Streaming continuous moisture and temperature telemetry would exhaust battery reserves within days. Hierarchical processing therefore keeps high-frequency sampling and basic anomaly detection on TinyML microcontrollers in sub-milliwatt power budgets. Local edge gateways aggregate measurements across clusters of probes and send only compressed, field-level summaries over cellular backhaul to cloud servers that schedule irrigation farm-wide.
  3. Wearable fitness tracking: A battery-powered smartwatch must keep its always-on sensing path averaging under \(1\text{ mW}\) to preserve battery life. The system employs hierarchical processing. Lightweight integer kernels run on an embedded microcontroller to track real-time motion and detect heart-rate thresholds. Intermediate biometric data synchronizes over Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE) to a paired smartphone, which uses mobile neural engines and gigabytes of DRAM to classify complex activity patterns. Finally, the smartphone transmits periodic compressed summaries over Wi-Fi or cellular networks to cloud infrastructure for longitudinal health modeling.
Checkpoint 2.3: Hybrid ML patterns

Hybrid architectures work when partitioning work across tiers, not when copying the same pipeline everywhere.

Integration patterns

Design sanity checks

Partitioning a workload across tiers works because each stage still answers to the same physical laws and software abstractions. Whether a stage executes on a coin-cell microcontroller or a megawatt cloud pod, it is governed by the same iron-law terms and maintained through the same engineering foundations.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why does serving always-on voice assistant traffic entirely in the cloud create an infrastructure and economic wall, and what does on-device wake-word detection actually remove?

    1. On-device wake-word detection removes the need to stream every microphone continuously to the cloud, so the data-center-count estimate applies to the remaining post-wake queries rather than to constant listening traffic.
    2. Once wake-word detection moves on device, all cloud serving disappears because speech recognition and language understanding no longer need remote models.
    3. The main problem is only that encrypted audio cannot be processed efficiently in cloud accelerators, so local wake detection solves a hardware compatibility issue rather than a traffic-scaling issue.
    4. Wake-word detection on device matters only for privacy; it does not materially change network load because continuous listening traffic and post-wake query traffic are the same scale.

  2. A smart agriculture system deploys soil moisture sensors on microcontrollers performing local anomaly detection, local gateway edge servers that aggregate sensor feeds across crop fields to trigger automated irrigation valves, and a central cloud system that aggregates regional data for seasonal crop yield forecasting. Which hybrid ML integration pattern does this architecture exemplify?

    1. Pure Train-Serve Split
    2. Monolithic Cloud Offload
    3. Hierarchical Processing
    4. Linear Single-Node Pipelining

  3. What is the primary operational failure risk in a hybrid ML system where preprocessing and feature extraction are split between client devices (mobile/edge) and centralized cloud servers?

    1. Transistors in edge devices reverse polarity when receiving cloud RPC payloads.
    2. The speed of light in optical fiber decreases proportionally with the number of connected edge clients.
    3. Edge devices become unable to allocate floating-point registers during network handshakes.
